# Foundations from first principles

This chapter teaches the minimum you need before any other chapter makes sense. It assumes no finance background. Each idea is taught once: a plain-English version first, then the mechanics a professional would use. Later chapters build on these ideas without repeating them.

Every number in an example is invented to show the arithmetic and is tagged *[Illustrative example]*. Nothing in this chapter describes Goldman Sachs's actual fees, clients or contracts.

For each important idea the chapter answers the same seven questions: what is it, why does it exist, who uses it, what actually happens, how does money move, what can go wrong, and why does it matter to a client coverage role.

## 1. Assets, liabilities and why anyone invests

### Assets and liabilities


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> An **asset** is something you own that is worth money or will pay you money: cash, a share in a company, a loan you have made to someone, a building.
> 
> A **liability** is something you owe: a mortgage, a bill, a promise to pay someone a pension for the rest of their life.
> 
> Almost every investor that an asset manager serves has both. The assets exist *because of* the liabilities. A pension scheme does not hold £1 billion for its own sake. It holds £1 billion because it has promised to pay pensions that will cost roughly that much.
> 
> This is the single most useful habit in this field: when you meet an investor, ask what they owe, to whom, and when. The answer explains most of what they buy.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> Professionals compare the two sides directly.
> 
> - **Funding level** = value of assets ÷ value of liabilities. A scheme with £950m of assets and £1,000m of liabilities is 95% funded *[Illustrative example]*.
> - Liabilities are future payments, so they must be converted into a value today. This is called **discounting**. The further away a payment is, and the higher the interest rate used (the **discount rate**), the less it is worth today.
> - That creates a non-obvious result: when long-term interest rates fall, the present value of long-dated liabilities rises, even though nobody has promised to pay a penny more. An investor can lose ground while its assets go up, if its liabilities go up faster.
> - **Asset–liability management (ALM)** is the discipline of choosing assets with the liabilities in mind rather than in isolation.



> **[Illustrative example: Why a "good year" can be a bad year]**
>
> A scheme's assets rise 6% in a year, from £1,000m to £1,060m. Over the same year long-term interest rates fall and the value placed on its pension promises rises 12%, from £1,000m to £1,120m. The funding level falls from 100% to about 95% (1,060 ÷ 1,120). The trustees are worse off against their real goal, despite a positive return. *[Illustrative example]*


### Who has money to invest, and why


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> Six kinds of owner appear again and again. What separates them is not how much money they have but what the money is *for*.


| Owner | Why the money exists | What it must pay, and when | What that usually demands |
|---|---|---|---|
| Individual savers | Retirement, a home, a safety net | Their own future spending; timing is personal | Simplicity, low cost, access |
| Pension schemes | To pay members' pensions | Monthly pensions for decades | Either matching promises (defined benefit) or growing pots (defined contribution) |
| Insurers | Premiums collected before claims are paid | Claims and annuity payments, under strict capital rules | Predictable income, high credit quality, capital efficiency |
| Endowments, foundations and charities | A gift meant to fund a mission indefinitely | Annual spending on the mission, ideally forever | Growth above inflation plus steady spending |
| Sovereign wealth funds | A state's surplus, often from oil, gas or trade | Varies: future generations, budget stabilisation, national development | Very long horizons, large size, access to private deals |
| Companies with cash | Money waiting to pay wages, suppliers, tax, dividends | Days to months | Safety and same-day access before return |


> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> The same grid tells you how each owner is governed, which matters as much as what it needs.
> 
> - A pension scheme is run by **trustees**, who have a legal duty to act for members and who usually meet only a few times a year.
> - An insurer is run by executives under a prudential regulator, with a chief investment officer and an investment committee.
> - A sovereign wealth fund (**SWF**) is run by professional staff under a state mandate.
> - A corporate treasury is run by a treasurer under a board-approved investment policy that typically lists what the cash may be placed in.
> 
> Who decides, how often they meet, and what they are personally accountable for shape the sales and service process far more than the product does. Chapter 3 returns to this client by client.



> **[Check your understanding: Test yourself]**
>
> 1. A charity must spend 4% of its endowment every year and wants the endowment to keep its real value forever. Inflation is 2.5%. Roughly what return does it need before costs, and why does that push it towards shares and away from cash? *[Illustrative example]*
> 2. Explain in two sentences how a pension scheme's funding level can fall in a year when its assets rose.
> 3. Which of the six owners above would care most about getting its money back the same day, and why?


## 2. The asset manager and the people around it

### What an asset manager is


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> An **asset manager** invests other people's money on their behalf, under instructions, for a fee.
> 
> Three points are easy to miss.
> 
> 1. **The money stays the client's.** The manager decides what to buy and sell. It does not own the assets, and they are held separately by a **custodian**, a bank whose job is safekeeping.
> 2. **The manager is an agent.** It acts for the client within limits the client sets. Gains and losses belong to the client.
> 3. **The fee is usually a small percentage of the money managed.** So the manager earns more when it manages more money and when markets rise, and less when clients leave or markets fall.
> 
> An analogy: you own a car and hire a professional driver. The car is still yours, kept in your garage (the custodian). You tell the driver where you need to go and which roads to avoid (the guidelines). Where the analogy stops working: a driver is paid by the hour, but an asset manager is paid as a share of the car's value, which gives it a commercial interest in the pot being large. That interest is usually aligned with yours, but not always, and Chapter 7 deals with the cases where it is not.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> **Why it exists.** Investing well needs scale, specialist skill, systems, market access and regulatory permissions that most asset owners cannot justify building. Pooling many clients spreads those fixed costs.
> 
> **How value moves.** The client pays a **management fee**, normally an annual percentage of the assets, accrued daily and billed monthly or quarterly. Some strategies add a **performance fee**. Trading costs, custody and administration are separate and are borne by the client's portfolio.
> 
> **What "assets under management" means.** **Assets under management (AUM)** is the total client money a firm manages. Some firms report a wider figure, **assets under supervision (AUS)**, which also counts assets where the firm earns a fee for advice or oversight without full discretion. Chapter 5 uses the exact definitions from Goldman Sachs's own filings.
> 
> **What can go wrong.** Underperformance against the agreed objective; breaching a guideline; operational errors in trading or reporting; a key investor leaving; or the manager growing a strategy beyond the size at which it can still be run well.
> 
> **Why it matters to this role.** Client coverage is the part of the asset manager that finds out what the client needs, brings the right internal specialists to the table, and remains accountable to the client afterwards. Coverage staff do not pick the investments. They are responsible for the client understanding what they have bought and for the firm understanding what the client actually needs.


### How an asset manager differs from its neighbours

Beginners often blur five different businesses. The cleanest test is to ask: *whose money is at risk, and what is the firm paid for?*

| Business | What it does | Whose money is at risk | How it is paid |
|---|---|---|---|
| Asset manager | Invests clients' money under a mandate | The client's | A percentage of assets, sometimes plus a performance fee |
| Bank (lending and investment banking) | Takes deposits, lends, advises companies on raising money and on deals | Partly its own balance sheet | Interest margin, advisory and underwriting fees |
| Broker or dealer (markets, "sales and trading") | Executes trades for investors and quotes prices | Its own capital when it takes the other side of a trade | Commissions and the gap between buying and selling prices |
| Investment consultant | Advises asset owners on strategy and on which managers to hire | Nobody's: it gives advice | Fees for advice, by retainer or project |
| Wealth adviser or wealth manager | Advises individuals and families and builds portfolios for them | The individual client's | Advice fees and a percentage of assets |


> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> The boundaries matter for this role in three practical ways.
> 
> - An asset manager is a **buy-side** firm: it buys investments for clients. Banks' markets divisions are the **sell side**: they sell trading and financing services *to* asset managers. A client coverage person at an asset manager is not a markets salesperson, even inside the same parent company.
> - The **investment consultant** is neither client nor competitor in the simple sense. It advises the client on whether to hire you, and often rates your strategies. That is why asset managers have a dedicated function called **consultant relations**.
> - A wealth manager can be a *client* of an asset manager. When a private bank selects a fund for its customers' portfolios, the asset manager is selling to a professional intermediary, not to the end saver. This is the business usually called third-party or wholesale distribution.


### The chain of people between a saver and an investment


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> The person whose retirement depends on the money almost never talks to the person investing it. In a UK pension scheme the chain typically looks like this:
> 
> **Member (beneficiary) → trustees → investment consultant → asset manager → markets**
> 
> Each link hires the next one to act on its behalf, and each link knows more about its own job than the one before it. Economists call this a **principal–agent chain**: a principal (who wants something done) relies on an agent (who does it) and cannot fully observe how well the agent is doing it.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> Each link has its own incentives and its own constraints.
> 
> - **Members** want the pension they were promised. They have almost no say in investment.
> - **Trustees** carry the legal responsibility. Many are not investment specialists. They must take proper advice and they are judged, in hindsight, on process as well as outcome.
> - **Consultants** advise on strategy and recommend managers. Their research ratings act as a gate: an unrated or poorly rated strategy may never reach the shortlist.
> - **Asset managers** want to win and keep assets, and are judged on performance, service and cost.
> 
> Because nobody in the chain can fully check the next link, the system relies on substitutes for direct observation: contracts, benchmarks, reporting, due diligence questionnaires, regulation, and reputation built over repeated dealings. Client coverage sits exactly at the joint between two links. Much of the job is reducing the information gap honestly, so that the principal can make a sound decision. Chapter 7 develops this at length.



> **[Check your understanding: Test yourself]**
>
> 1. A friend says "asset managers gamble with other people's money and keep the winnings". Correct the three errors in that sentence.
> 2. Why might a consultant be more important to winning a pension mandate than the trustees' personal impression of the manager?
> 3. In the driver analogy, who is the custodian, and why does it matter that it is a separate party?


## 3. How money is handed over: funds, mandates and the contract

### Pooled funds and segregated mandates


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> There are two basic ways to give an asset manager money.
> 
> - A **pooled fund** is a shared pot. Many investors buy units or shares in one fund, the manager runs one portfolio, and everyone gets the same strategy and the same rules. The value of one unit is the **net asset value (NAV)** per unit.
> - A **segregated mandate** (also called a separate account) is a private pot. One client's assets are held in that client's own custody account and managed to rules written for that client alone.
> 
> A pooled fund is like taking a scheduled coach: cheaper, runs on a fixed route, leaves whether or not you are on it. A segregated mandate is like chartering your own coach: you set the route, but you need enough passengers to justify it. The analogy fails in one respect: in a pooled fund, other investors' behaviour can affect you, because large withdrawals by others can force the fund to sell assets.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> | Feature | Pooled fund | Segregated mandate |
> |---|---|---|
> | Ownership | Units in a fund vehicle | The underlying securities directly |
> | Customisation | None beyond choosing a share class | Benchmark, exclusions, risk limits, reporting all negotiable |
> | Typical minimum | Low | High, often tens or hundreds of millions, because of fixed set-up cost |
> | Documents | Prospectus and application form | A negotiated investment management agreement |
> | Set-up effort | Days | Weeks to months: legal negotiation, custody account openings market by market, operational testing |
> | Fee | Published by share class; large investors may get a cheaper class or a rebate | Individually negotiated, often tiered |
> 
> In Europe and the UK the most common regulated pooled fund for general distribution is the **UCITS** (Undertakings for Collective Investment in Transferable Securities), a fund built to a common rulebook on diversification, liquidity and disclosure. A fund's annual running cost to the investor is summarised as the **ongoing charges figure (OCF)**.
> 
> **What can go wrong.** In pooled funds: dealing restrictions or suspension if the assets cannot be sold as fast as investors want out. In segregated mandates: slow onboarding, a guideline written ambiguously, or a market the custodian has not opened in time for the first trade.
> 
> **Why it matters to this role.** The vehicle changes the work. Selling a fund to a wealth intermediary is about getting an existing product approved and then used. Winning a segregated institutional mandate is a negotiated project with legal, operational and investment workstreams that client coverage must coordinate.


### The investment management agreement and guidelines


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> A segregated mandate runs on a contract called the **investment management agreement (IMA)**. Attached to it are the **investment guidelines**: the client's written instructions about what the manager is trying to achieve and what it may not do.
> 
> The guidelines are the practical answer to the principal–agent problem. The client cannot watch every trade, so it writes down the boundaries in advance and asks for regular proof that they were respected.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> A typical IMA and its schedules cover:
> 
> - **Objective and benchmark**: for example, outperform a named index by 1% a year over rolling three-year periods, before fees *[Illustrative example]*.
> - **Permitted and prohibited investments**: asset types, credit quality floors, country or sector limits, exclusions.
> - **Risk limits**: maximum position sizes, tracking error range, use of derivatives.
> - **Fees**: the rate, any tiers, any performance fee and how it is calculated.
> - **Reporting and meetings**: what the client receives, how often.
> - **Termination**: notice period, often short. Many institutional mandates can be ended on roughly a month's notice or less.
> - **Standard of care and liability**: what the manager is responsible for if it breaches.
> 
> Two consequences follow. First, a mandate is recurring revenue but not guaranteed revenue: the client can usually leave quickly. Second, a breach of guidelines, even one that made money, is a serious event that must be reported to the client, because the client did not consent to that risk.



> **[Illustrative example: A guideline in practice]**
>
> A mandate says: "No more than 5% of the portfolio in any one issuer." A bond the portfolio holds is upgraded and rallies; a second bond from the same issuer is already held. Together they drift to 5.3% of the portfolio without a single trade. That is a *passive* breach. The portfolio team must bring it back within the limit in an agreed time, and the client is told. The coverage person does not fix the breach; they make sure the client hears about it promptly, in plain terms, with the remedy and the date. *[Illustrative example]*



> **[Check your understanding: Test yourself]**
>
> 1. A client with £40m asks for a segregated mandate with a custom exclusion list. Give two reasons the manager might suggest a pooled fund instead. *[Illustrative example]*
> 2. Why is "the mandate can be terminated on 30 days' notice" important for understanding what a relationship manager does all year?
> 3. A breach made the client money. Why must it still be reported?


## 4. Measuring the job: benchmarks, risk, liquidity and time

### Benchmarks, and active versus passive


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> A **benchmark** is the yardstick a portfolio is measured against, usually a market **index**: a published list of securities and their weights, such as the largest listed companies in a country.
> 
> - A **passive** (or index) manager tries to *match* the benchmark as closely and cheaply as possible.
> - An **active** manager tries to *beat* it by choosing differently, and charges more for trying.
> 
> The extra return above the benchmark is called **alpha**. The return you get simply from being exposed to the market is called **beta**. Beta is cheap to buy. Alpha is uncertain and is what active fees pay for.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> - Active returns across all investors sum to roughly the market return before costs, so the *average* active manager, after fees, lags the index. That arithmetic is the source of the long-running shift towards passive and of pressure on active fees.
> - Active management is easier to defend where indexes are hard to replicate or where the benchmark itself is flawed: much of fixed income, smaller companies, and private markets where no investable index exists.
> - A benchmark can be the client's own liabilities instead of a market index. Then "doing well" means keeping pace with what the client owes, not beating other managers.
> - **Choosing the benchmark is a client decision with consequences.** A manager measured against the wrong yardstick can look brilliant or terrible for reasons that have nothing to do with skill. Coverage staff are often the people who notice the benchmark no longer fits the client's situation.


### Risk, four ways


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> "Risk" means different things to different clients. Four measures cover most conversations.
> 
> 1. **Volatility**: how much returns bounce around from period to period.
> 2. **Drawdown**: the fall from a peak to the next low. This is what loss feels like.
> 3. **Tracking error**: how differently the portfolio behaves from its benchmark. Low tracking error means it hugs the index.
> 4. **Liability-relative risk**: how much the gap between assets and liabilities can move. For a pension scheme this is the risk that matters most.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> - Volatility is usually quoted as an annualised standard deviation. A fund with 10% volatility would be expected to land within about ±10% of its average return in roughly two years out of three, if returns behaved like a bell curve, which they do not reliably do *[Illustrative example]*.
> - Tracking error is the volatility of the *difference* between portfolio and benchmark returns. A mandate targeting 1% outperformance with 2% tracking error is asking for an **information ratio** of 0.5 *[Illustrative example]*.
> - A "safe" asset on one measure can be risky on another. Cash has almost no volatility, but for a pension scheme with 20-year promises, holding cash is a large liability-relative risk, because cash does not rise in value when the liabilities do.
> - Other risks sit alongside these: **credit risk** (a borrower fails to pay), **counterparty risk** (the other side of a contract fails), **concentration**, **currency**, and **operational risk**.



> **[Illustrative example: The same portfolio, two verdicts]**
>
> A portfolio of long-dated government bonds falls 20% in a year when interest rates rise sharply. For a saver who needs the money next year, that is a disaster. For a pension scheme whose liabilities also fell about 20% for the same reason, the funding level barely moved, which is exactly what the portfolio was hired to do. *[Illustrative example]*


### Liquidity


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> **Liquidity** is how quickly you can turn an investment into cash without accepting a poor price. Shares in large companies: seconds. A commercial building: months. A commitment to a private equity fund: usually years, and not at a time of your choosing.
> 
> Investors are normally paid extra for accepting illiquidity. The danger is needing cash at the moment the asset cannot be sold.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> - **Liquidity mismatch** is the classic failure: a fund promises daily dealing but owns assets that take weeks to sell. If many investors ask to leave at once, the fund may have to suspend dealing.
> - Institutions run a **liquidity budget**: how much of the portfolio can be locked up, given the cash they must pay out and the collateral they may be called on to post.
> - Liquidity needs change. A pension scheme preparing to transfer its liabilities to an insurer suddenly cares far more about being able to sell, which can make previously sensible illiquid holdings a problem.


### Investment horizons


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> The **investment horizon** is how long the money can stay invested before it is needed. A long horizon lets an investor ride out falls and hold illiquid assets. A short horizon does not.
> 
> Horizon comes from the liabilities, not from preference. An endowment may think in decades; a corporate treasurer in days.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> The stated horizon and the *governance* horizon often differ. Trustees with a 30-year liability may still review a manager every quarter and lose patience after three poor years. A good coverage professional keeps the client's attention on the horizon the strategy was designed for, and also says so plainly when underperformance has gone beyond what the strategy's design can explain.


### Public and private markets


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> **Public markets** are where securities trade openly with visible prices: listed shares and most bonds. **Private markets** are investments negotiated directly and not traded on an exchange: private equity (buying companies), private credit (lending directly), infrastructure, real estate.
> 
> Private assets can offer returns or income that public markets do not, in exchange for illiquidity, less frequent valuation and higher fees.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> Private market funds work differently from ordinary funds, and the difference changes the fee arithmetic in Chapter 5.
> 
> - The investor makes a **commitment** (a promise of, say, £50m *[Illustrative example]*). The manager **calls** the money in stages over several years as it finds deals, and returns it through **distributions** as investments are sold.
> - Management fees are often charged on **committed capital** during the investment period and on invested capital afterwards, not on NAV.
> - The manager typically also earns **carried interest**: a share of profits, often around a fifth, payable only after investors have received their money back plus a minimum return called the **hurdle** *[Illustrative example]*.
> - Valuations are estimates made quarterly, so reported volatility understates the real economic risk.



> **[Check your understanding: Test yourself]**
>
> 1. Why can the *average* active manager not beat the index after fees? Where is that argument weakest?
> 2. Give an example of an asset that is low-risk for one client and high-risk for another, and say which risk measure explains the difference.
> 3. A fund offers daily dealing and holds office buildings. What is the risk called and what might happen in a crisis?
> 4. A client commits £50m to a private equity fund charging 1.5% on committed capital. What is the first-year fee, and why is it not based on NAV? *[Illustrative example]*


## 5. How the manager gets paid: basis points and fees

### Basis points


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> Fees in asset management are small percentages, so the industry counts in **basis points (bps)**. One basis point is one hundredth of one percent.
> 
> - 1 bp = 0.01%
> - 100 bps = 1%
> - 25 bps = 0.25%
> 
> The arithmetic you will use constantly:
> 
> **Annual fee = assets × fee rate**
> 
> £500 million at 20 bps = £500,000,000 × 0.0020 = £1,000,000 a year. *[Illustrative example]*



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> Four refinements turn the simple sum into what actually gets billed.
> 
> 1. **Fees accrue on average assets over time, not on the opening balance.** If the £500m arrives halfway through the year, the first-year fee is about half. If markets fall 10% the day after funding, the fee base falls with them.
> 2. **Tiered fees (breakpoints).** Larger mandates pay a lower rate on each additional slice. Example: 25 bps on the first £200m and 15 bps on the rest. On £500m that is £500,000 + £450,000 = £950,000, an *effective* rate of 19 bps *[Illustrative example]*.
> 3. **Performance fees.** A share of the return above a benchmark or hurdle, often with a **high-water mark** so the manager is not paid twice for recovering an earlier loss.
> 4. **Most-favoured-nation (MFN) clauses.** A promise that the client's fee will be no worse than that of any comparable client. One discount can therefore reprice several relationships.
> 
> In pooled funds the investor sees an all-in ongoing charge deducted inside the fund. Different **share classes** of the same fund carry different charges for different types of investor. Where an intermediary or large investor negotiates a lower price, it is often delivered through a cheaper share class or a **rebate**.



> **[Limits and caveats: Revenue is not profit, and recurring is not guaranteed]**
>
> A management fee is the manager's *gross* revenue. Out of it come portfolio management, trading, technology, operations, compliance, distribution and overheads. And because most mandates can be ended at short notice and the fee base moves with markets, a fee that recurs is not a fee that is guaranteed. Chapter 5 works this through.


**Why it matters to this role.** Every commercial judgement in client coverage, such as whether a mandate is worth a custom build, what a fee concession really costs, or what a client's partial withdrawal means, starts with this arithmetic. You are expected to do it quickly and to state your assumptions.


> **[Check your understanding: Test yourself]**
>
> 1. Convert 37.5 bps to a percentage and calculate the annual fee on £80m. *[Illustrative example]*
> 2. A mandate charges 30 bps on the first £100m and 20 bps above that. What is the annual fee and effective rate on £250m? *[Illustrative example]*
> 3. A £1bn mandate at 15 bps funds on 1 October. Roughly what fee is earned in that calendar year, ignoring market moves? *[Illustrative example]*
> 4. Why might a manager refuse a fee cut that a single client "obviously" deserves?


## 6. Who the clients are, and why coverage exists

### Institutional, intermediary and retail


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> An asset manager's clients fall into three broad groups, and each is reached in a different way.
> 
> - **Institutional clients** invest large sums on behalf of an organisation and its beneficiaries: pension schemes, insurers, endowments, sovereign funds, central banks, corporates.
> - **Intermediaries** are professional firms that choose investments for *their* clients: private banks, wealth managers, financial advisers, fund platforms, funds of funds. The asset manager's customer is the intermediary; the money belongs to the intermediary's clients.
> - **Retail investors** are individuals investing for themselves, usually through an adviser or an online platform.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> | | Institutional | Intermediary (wholesale) | Retail |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Who decides | Trustees, investment committee, CIO, often on a consultant's advice | A fund selection team (the **gatekeeper**), then individual advisers or portfolio managers | The individual, or their adviser |
> | Typical vehicle | Segregated mandate or institutional fund | Pooled fund, model portfolio, ETF | Pooled fund via a platform |
> | Size per decision | Large and lumpy | Starts small, builds as advisers adopt | Small |
> | Selection process | Formal: request for proposal, due diligence, presentations | Fund research, approved list (**buy list**), then internal promotion | Brand, ratings, platform availability |
> | What "winning" means | Appointment, then funding | Approval, then *usage*: being on the list is permission to be bought, not a purchase | Net sales |
> 
> Two terms you will meet immediately:
> 
> - A **request for proposal (RFP)** is a formal invitation to bid for a mandate, with a long list of questions about the firm, team, process, performance, risk controls and fees.
> - A **due diligence questionnaire (DDQ)** is a detailed questionnaire used to verify a manager before or after appointment. Answering RFPs and DDQs accurately and consistently is a core, unglamorous work product of any client business.


### What "distribution" or client coverage is


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> **Distribution** is the industry's word for everything involved in getting an asset manager's capabilities to clients and keeping those clients: finding prospects, understanding needs, proposing solutions, winning mandates, onboarding, reporting, and maintaining the relationship. Firms use different names for the department: client coverage, client solutions, client business, sales and client service.
> 
> Why does it need to exist at all? Why can't good investment performance sell itself?



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> Four reasons, each tied to something already taught.
> 
> 1. **Search costs.** There are thousands of strategies. An asset owner cannot evaluate them all, and a portfolio manager cannot find and visit every potential client. Someone has to match need to capability.
> 2. **Information asymmetry.** The client cannot see inside the investment process. Coverage translates it, answers the hard questions, and brings the right specialist. The client's own protection against being misled is formal due diligence and, often, a consultant.
> 3. **The need is rarely "a product".** A scheme's real problem might be a funding gap, a cash-flow shortfall or a governance burden. Working out what is being asked for, and whether the firm has an honest answer, is skilled work.
> 4. **Mandates are terminable and needs change.** Someone must own the relationship after the sale: reviews, problems, changes in the client's situation, and the decision about what to propose next, or whether to propose anything.
> 
> **How money moves.** Coverage produces no investment return. Its commercial contribution is to the *quantity and durability* of fee-paying assets: new assets won, existing assets retained, and relationships widened. Those outcomes are produced jointly with investment teams, product specialists, legal and operations, so attributing a win to one person or function is always partly arbitrary.
> 
> **What can go wrong.** Selling something unsuitable; over-promising what a strategy can do; slow or inaccurate answers; a relationship that depends on one individual; or winning business the firm cannot serve well.
> 
> **The usual split.** Many firms divide coverage into **sales** (new clients and new assets) and **relationship management** (existing clients), with **consultant relations**, **product or investment specialists** and **RFP teams** alongside. How strict that split is varies by firm and team. Chapter 6 examines it without assuming a universal rule.



> **[Check your understanding: Test yourself]**
>
> 1. A fund is added to a private bank's approved list. Why has no money necessarily moved yet?
> 2. Give one reason, in your own words, why excellent performance alone does not win an institutional mandate.
> 3. What does coverage contribute commercially if it produces no investment return? Why is it hard to credit that contribution to one person?


## 7. Four client problems you will meet constantly

### Defined benefit and defined contribution pensions


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> A workplace pension is one of two types.
> 
> - **Defined benefit (DB)**: the *pension* is promised. "You will receive a fraction of your salary for each year worked, for life." The employer bears the risk. If the investments fall short, the employer must pay in more.
> - **Defined contribution (DC)**: the *payments in* are defined. You and your employer contribute to a pot in your name, and what you retire on depends on how that pot grows. The member bears the risk.
> 
> DB is a promise and therefore a liability to be matched. DC is a savings pot to be grown. They are different investment problems and are sold to and served differently.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> **UK DB today.** The Pension Protection Fund's *Purple Book 2025* records 4,840 eligible private-sector DB schemes with about 8.6 million members at 31 March 2025, of whom only around 0.7 million are still building up benefits. Schemes held 71% of assets in bonds and bond proxies, and in aggregate showed a surplus of £214 billion on the PPF's own measure, a funding ratio of 125% *[Verified]* [W1-S01]. In short: UK DB is large, mostly closed, mature, heavily in bonds, and in aggregate well funded on that measure. The PPF measure values a reduced level of benefits, so it flatters funding compared with the cost of insuring the full promises.
> 
> That maturity sets the agenda. A well-funded closed scheme is asking about its **endgame**: transfer the liabilities to an insurer through a **buy-in** (the scheme buys an insurance policy as an asset) or a **buyout** (the insurer takes over the promises and the scheme winds up), or **run on** and manage the scheme for the long term.
> 
> **Who governs.** Trustees run the scheme. **The Pensions Regulator (TPR)** describes itself as "the public body that protects workplace pensions in the UK" *[Verified]* [W1-S02]. The strength of the employer's ability and willingness to support the scheme is called the **covenant**.
> 
> **UK DC today.** Employers must automatically enrol eligible workers, with a legal minimum total contribution of 8% of qualifying earnings, at least 3% of it from the employer *[Supported inference]* [W1-S07]. Most members never make an investment choice and sit in the scheme's **default fund**, so the commercial decision is made by whoever designs the default: a master trust, an insurer or the employer's trustees. DC is growing, is very price-sensitive, and is where the policy debate about investing in private markets is concentrated. Chapter 9 covers the current reforms.
> 
> **Public-sector DB.** The **Local Government Pension Scheme (LGPS)** in England and Wales is funded DB, run through 86 local administering authorities that invest through shared asset pools, which government policy is consolidating from eight to six *[Supported inference]* [W1-S08]. For an external manager the buyer is increasingly the pool, not the individual fund.
> 
> **Why it matters to this role.** "A UK pension client" is at least four different clients: a closed corporate DB scheme heading for insurance, a DB scheme choosing to run on, a DC default designer, and an LGPS pool. A proposal that suits one can be irrelevant to another.


### Liability-driven investment (LDI) in plain English


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> A DB scheme's biggest risk is not a stock market fall. It is that the value of its promises rises because long-term interest rates fall or expected inflation rises.
> 
> **Liability-driven investment (LDI)** means holding assets that move in step with the liabilities, so that when the liabilities go up, the assets go up too. The natural matching assets are long-dated government bonds (in the UK, **gilts**), including **index-linked gilts** whose payments rise with inflation.
> 
> Think of a fixed-rate mortgage. You fix the rate not because you predict rates will rise but because you do not want your finances to depend on it. LDI is the same idea in reverse for a scheme: removing a risk it is not paid to take. The analogy stops being accurate once leverage is involved, as explained below.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> **The complication: leverage.** A scheme that is not fully funded cannot afford to put everything in gilts; it also needs growth assets to close the gap. So many schemes obtained gilt exposure *with borrowed money*, through gilt repurchase agreements (**repo**) or interest rate swaps. £1 of capital might support £2 to £4 of hedging *[Illustrative example]*. That freed capital for growth assets.
> 
> **How money moves.** Leveraged positions require **collateral**. When gilt yields rise (prices fall), the LDI portfolio must post more collateral, quickly. The scheme is not worse off overall, because its liabilities have fallen as well, but it needs *cash now*.
> 
> **What went wrong in 2022.** After the UK government's fiscal statement in late September 2022, long gilt yields rose faster than LDI funds could raise collateral. The Bank of England describes funds having to deleverage by selling gilts into a falling market, "and an adverse feedback loop took hold". The Bank bought £19.3 billion of gilts between 28 September and 14 October 2022 to restore order *[Verified]* [W1-S05]. The lesson the industry took was about liquidity and leverage, not about hedging itself: LDI portfolios now run with larger collateral buffers and clearer plans for where emergency cash comes from.
> 
> **Why it matters to this role.** LDI is central to UK DB relationships. Even a junior person must be able to explain why a scheme's assets falling 25% was not necessarily bad news, why the *speed* of a collateral call matters, and why a client's other holdings are judged partly on how quickly they could be sold to support the hedge.


### Money market funds in plain English


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> A **money market fund (MMF)** is a pooled fund that invests in very short-term, high-quality debt, such as government bills and bank deposits maturing within days or weeks. Its job is to keep cash safe, available the same day, and earning roughly the going short-term interest rate.
> 
> Who uses them? Anyone holding large amounts of cash that would be unwise to leave with a single bank: company treasurers, pension schemes holding collateral, insurers, other asset managers.
> 
> It behaves like a shared current account spread across many borrowers. The analogy stops being accurate in one important respect: an MMF is an investment, not a deposit. No deposit guarantee stands behind it and its value is not promised.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> **Why it exists.** A treasurer with £300m cannot sensibly leave it with one bank: if that bank fails, the money is an unsecured claim. An MMF spreads the cash across dozens of issuers, with professional credit analysis and daily access.
> 
> **How money moves.** The investor earns the fund's **yield** minus a fee, typically a few to a couple of dozen basis points. The fee is small, so the business depends on very large balances, operational reliability and treasury-system connectivity, not on outperformance.
> 
> **The three types.** UK and EU rules recognise three structures: public debt constant NAV (**CNAV**), low volatility NAV (**LVNAV**) and variable NAV (**VNAV**) *[Supported inference]* [W1-S09]. An LVNAV fund may deal at a stable price of 1.00 per share only while the true market value of its assets stays close: if the gap exceeds 20 basis points, dealing must switch to the market-based price *[Verified]* [W1-S06].
> 
> **What can go wrong.** A run: many investors redeem at once, as in March 2020, and the fund must sell assets to pay them. Regulation therefore sets minimum holdings of assets maturing within a day and a week.
> 
> **Why it matters to this role.** Liquidity clients are a distinct business. The client's questions are about safety, access, cut-off times, concentration and their own investment policy limits. Balances move with the client's business cycle and with interest rates, so "flows" here mean something different from winning a long-term mandate.


### OCIO and fiduciary management in plain English


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> Running an institutional portfolio involves dozens of decisions: how much in each asset class, which managers, when to rebalance, how to hedge, how to report. Many governing bodies meet quarterly and are not full-time investors.
> 
> An **outsourced chief investment officer (OCIO)** arrangement hands those day-to-day decisions to a specialist provider. The governing body keeps the big decisions (objectives, risk appetite, the journey plan) and delegates implementation. In the UK pensions market the usual name is **fiduciary management (FM)**. TPR's guidance describes the term as generally used for "a governance model through which trustees delegate" investment decisions to a provider *[Verified]* [W1-S04].
> 
> It is like hiring a general contractor to build a house instead of hiring and coordinating each tradesperson yourself. The analogy stops being accurate at one point that matters: a fiduciary manager may place some of the money in its *own* funds, which creates a conflict of interest that a general contractor does not normally have.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> **How it differs from an ordinary mandate.** An ordinary mandate is one slice of the portfolio run to a benchmark. An OCIO mandate covers the whole portfolio, or a large part of it, and is measured against the client's own objective, typically the liabilities. The provider usually selects *other* managers as well as, or instead of, using its own strategies.
> 
> **How money moves.** The client pays a fee for the fiduciary service, plus the fees of the underlying managers and funds. Scale lets the provider negotiate lower underlying fees, which is part of the case for the model. The fee rate on the whole portfolio is low compared with an active mandate, but the asset base is very large and the relationship is deep.
> 
> **Safeguards.** Because delegation is broad, UK regulation requires process. Following a Competition and Markets Authority investigation and its 2019 Order *[Supported inference]* [W1-S03], trustees must run a qualifying competitive tender, inviting bids from at least three unconnected providers, before fiduciary management first covers 20% or more of scheme assets *[Verified]* [W1-S04]. Many schemes also hire an independent third-party evaluator to help select and then monitor the fiduciary manager.
> 
> **What can go wrong.** Unclear division of responsibility between trustees and provider; conflicts over use of in-house products; difficulty comparing performance across providers; and a costly, slow exit if the relationship fails.
> 
> **Variants.** Mandates range from a small scheme using a provider's standard pooled solution to a very large scheme with a bespoke arrangement. In some large cases the provider takes on the client's existing in-house investment staff. That is one model, not the definition of OCIO.
> 
> **Why it matters to this role.** OCIO changes the nature of the sale and the relationship. The sale is a governance decision as much as an investment decision, often run as a formal tender with an evaluator. The relationship afterwards is closer to a partnership with the trustee board than to reporting on a single strategy.



> **[Check your understanding: Test yourself]**
>
> 1. Who bears the investment risk in DB, and who in DC? Why does that change who the asset manager needs to persuade?
> 2. Explain to a non-specialist why UK pension schemes were selling gilts in September 2022 even though higher yields improved their funding.
> 3. Why would a corporate treasurer use a money market fund instead of a bank deposit? Name one way the fund is *less* safe than a deposit.
> 4. What conflict of interest can arise in fiduciary management, and name one safeguard that exists to manage it.


## 8. Putting it together

One short story uses almost everything in this chapter. All figures are invented. *[Illustrative example]*

A closed UK defined benefit scheme has £800m of assets and liabilities valued at £780m. It is advised by an investment consultant. Its trustees meet quarterly.

- **The need (section 1).** The scheme is slightly over-funded and wants to keep it that way while it decides between insuring and running on. Its problem is liability-relative risk and liquidity, not maximum return.
- **The chain (section 2).** The trustees decide. The consultant frames the options and shortlists managers. Members never appear in the room.
- **The vehicle and contract (section 3).** The trustees want a £200m segregated credit mandate designed to produce the cash the scheme needs to pay pensions each year. The guidelines set a minimum credit quality and limit how much can be in any one borrower.
- **The yardstick (section 4).** Success is defined as delivering the scheduled cash flows with few defaults, not as beating a bond index. The portfolio must be saleable to an insurer later, so illiquid holdings are capped.
- **The fee (section 5).** At 18 bps the mandate would generate about £360,000 a year of gross revenue for the manager if fully funded for a full year, before costs.
- **The coverage work (section 6).** A salesperson and the consultant relations team make sure the consultant understands the strategy. An RFP is answered. A portfolio manager presents. Legal negotiates the IMA. After funding, a relationship manager owns quarterly reporting and is the first call when something changes.
- **What changes (section 7).** Two years later the trustees agree a buy-in with an insurer. The mandate is transferred or wound down. The manager's revenue from this client falls, possibly to zero, and that is the right outcome for the members.

That last line is deliberate. Understanding this business includes understanding that the best result for the client sometimes reduces the manager's assets. The chapters that follow examine what client coverage does about that, honestly.


> **[Check your understanding: Final self-test]**
>
> Without looking back, explain to an imaginary friend who has never studied finance:
> 1. What an asset manager is paid for, and by what arithmetic.
> 2. Why a pension scheme is two different clients depending on whether it is DB or DC.
> 3. Why an asset manager needs people whose job is not investing.
> 4. One situation in which the right advice loses the manager money.


---

# Sales versus relationship management: what actually differs

## How to read this chapter

*[Layer B · interpretation]* Almost everything in this chapter is a description of **general industry practice** in institutional and wholesale asset management distribution. It is not a disclosed description of how Goldman Sachs Asset Management (GSAM) organises its people. Treat the whole chapter as *[Supported inference]* unless a sentence carries a different badge. Three kinds of statement appear:

- **Regulatory facts**, cited to the regulator or the legislation and badged individually.
- **The former intern's labels** for the two sides of a team. These are *[Unverified account]* and are discussed, not relied on.
- **Illustrative days, clients and work products**. Every client named in an example is fictional and every figure is *[Illustrative example]*.


> **[Limits and caveats: What this chapter cannot tell you]**
>
> It cannot tell you which side of which team you would join, how Goldman Sachs splits credit between sales and relationship management, or what either side is paid. Chapter 2 holds the verified programme facts and Chapter 4 audits the former intern's claims. Use this chapter to understand the work, then test it against what interviewers and team members actually say.


## The short version


> **[Level 1 · First principles]**
>
> An asset manager is paid a fee on the money it looks after. Two things therefore matter commercially: **new money arriving** and **existing money staying**. Most distribution teams divide their people along that line.
> 
> - **Sales** (also called business development) spends most of its time on organisations that are *not yet* clients, or on new mandates from existing ones. The typical output is a proposal that leads to an appointment.
> - **Relationship management (RM)** spends most of its time on organisations that *already are* clients. The typical output is a client who understands what they own, gets answers quickly, and has a sound basis for deciding whether to stay, add or leave.
> 
> That is a useful first sketch. It becomes misleading if you take it literally, because the same person often does both, because the most valuable "sale" is frequently an existing client adding money, and because neither side makes the investment decisions that determine whether the client is ultimately satisfied.



> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> A more precise way to state the split: both roles are **coverage** roles, meaning each person is accountable for a named list of organisations. They differ in *where the list sits in the lifecycle*. Sales lists are dominated by prospects and open searches, so the work is lumpy, forward-looking and judged on conversion. RM lists are dominated by funded mandates, so the work is periodic (reporting cycles, review meetings, contract events) and judged on retention, service quality and growth of the existing book. The boundary between "new" and "existing" is set by each firm's coverage model and its credit-allocation rules, and those rules differ by firm, region and client segment.


## Where the labels come from, and why they are unreliable

The former intern used "CSG" for the sales side and "CCG (Client Coverage Group)" for the relationship side within a team, while also using "Client Solutions Group" for the whole umbrella. *[Unverified account]* That is one person's shorthand from one team in one year. It may be accurate for that desk, it may be informal desk language, or it may have changed. Chapter 2 records what official sources establish about the names.

Three reasons to hold any label loosely:

1. **Titles are not standardised across the industry.** The person who retains clients may be called a relationship manager, client director, client portfolio manager, client advisor, account manager or client executive. The person who wins them may be called sales, business development, client solutions, distribution or coverage. Two firms can use the same title for different jobs.
2. **"Solutions" has at least three meanings.** It can mean the whole client-facing group, a team that designs multi-asset or liability-driven portfolios, or a marketing word for "we sell more than one product". Ask which one a speaker means.
3. **A label describes an organisational chart, and the work follows the client.** If a pension scheme's trustees phone the person they know, that person handles the call whatever their business card says.

A safe way to speak about this in an interview is to describe the *activity* ("covering prospective UK pension clients" or "servicing existing mandates") and let the interviewer supply their own team's name for it.

## What the two sides have in common

Before the differences, the shared ground, which is larger than most candidates expect:

- **Neither side manages money.** Portfolio managers decide what to buy and sell. Coverage professionals explain, represent and coordinate.
- **Both are regulated communicators.** In the UK, a firm's communications with clients must be "fair, clear and not misleading" under the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) Conduct of Business Sourcebook (COBS) rule 4.2.1R. *[Supported inference]* [W3-S06] Anything either side sends a client normally passes through compliance-approved templates or review.
- **Both depend on the same specialists**: portfolio managers, product or investment specialists, the request-for-proposal (RFP) team, legal, onboarding, operations, performance and reporting.
- **Both use the same client record.** Meeting notes, pipeline entries and contact maps live in a customer relationship management (CRM) system. The quality of those records is a junior responsibility on both sides.
- **Both are judged over long periods.** A mandate can take a year or more to win and can stay for a decade. Neither side sees quick feedback on most of its work.

## The comparison in detail

The tables below split the comparison into groups so they stay readable. "Where it blurs" is the column to study: it shows why a clean split rarely survives contact with a real client.

### Purpose and commercial responsibility

| Dimension | Sales | Relationship management | Where it blurs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Core objective | Bring in new mandates and new client organisations | Keep existing mandates well served and appropriately sized | Extra money from an existing client can be led by either side |
| Commercial responsibility | New fee-paying assets, usually measured as gross or net new flows | The existing book: retention, renewal, growth, sometimes profitability | Credit for one outcome is often shared, with rules that differ by firm |
| Targets | Flows, pipeline value, number of qualified opportunities, meetings with target accounts | Retention, client satisfaction, review-cycle completion, growth of the book | Many firms give RM a new-business target and sales a retention interest |
| Typical time horizon | A search from first contact to funding | The life of the mandate, including re-tenders | Long sales cycles make "sales" feel like RM for a client that has not paid yet |

### Daily work

| Dimension | Sales | Relationship management | Where it blurs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Daily activities | Prospect research, outreach, discovery meetings, proposal and RFP coordination, pitch rehearsal, pipeline updates | Reporting oversight, review meetings, query handling, change requests, contract and fee events, escalation of problems | Both prepare and follow up meetings and both chase internal teams |
| Work products | Prospect briefing, capability pitch, RFP response, pricing proposal, pipeline report | Quarterly review pack, meeting minutes and action log, client service plan, breach or incident explanation, renewal paper | A review pack for a client who is considering a second mandate is a sales document in all but name |
| Client contact | Many first conversations, often with people who owe you nothing | Fewer organisations, repeated contact, wider range of contacts inside each | Senior decision-makers meet both |
| Rhythm | Irregular, driven by searches and deadlines set by the client or its consultant | Regular, driven by reporting dates and governance calendars | Problems arrive unscheduled on both sides |

### Knowledge and coordination

| Dimension | Sales | Relationship management | Where it blurs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Investment knowledge | Broad: enough across many strategies to diagnose a need and bring the right specialist | Deep on what the client holds: guidelines, benchmark, attribution, risk limits, fee schedule | Both must explain a strategy accurately without overstating it |
| Client knowledge | Wide map of a market: who is searching, who advises them, when contracts end | Deep map of each client: governance, personalities, history, sensitivities | RM knowledge is the best source of sales intelligence about that client |
| Internal coordination | Assembling a pitch team and a response under deadline | Getting operations, legal, reporting and portfolio managers to deliver over years | Onboarding sits exactly on the seam and is where handovers fail |
| Negotiation | Fees, terms and guidelines before appointment, within limits approved internally | Fee reviews, guideline changes and service levels during the mandate | Neither side sets price alone; pricing normally needs approval |

### Pressure and progression

| Dimension | Sales | Relationship management | Where it blurs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pressure | Visible, numerical, binary: the mandate was won or it was not | Diffuse and continuous: many small obligations, occasional crises | A lost client is as visible as a lost pitch |
| Typical frustration | Months of work ending in a decision you cannot influence further | Being accountable for outcomes (performance, errors) you did not produce | Both explain decisions made by other people |
| Progression | Larger territories, bigger prospects, team leadership | Larger or more complex clients, senior client director roles, team leadership | People move between the two, and senior roles usually combine them |
| Compensation | See the evidence note below | See the evidence note below | Depends on firm, seniority and credit rules |

<!-- COMPENSATION-EVIDENCE -->


> **[Limits and caveats: Reading compensation claims]**
>
> Pay differences between two roles tell you how one firm prices two sets of tasks in one period. They are not evidence about which role is more valuable, more skilled or a better fit for you. A difference in base salary at junior level, if it exists, may reflect seat scarcity, a variable-pay philosophy or simple history. Do not build a preference for sales or RM on an unverified pay figure.


### The same comparison, in mechanics


> **[Level 2 · Institutional mechanics]**
>
> **Objectives.** A sales objective is naturally expressed as a *flow*: assets that arrive in a period. An RM objective is naturally expressed as a *stock*: assets and revenue that remain. Flows are volatile and easy to attribute to a moment. Stocks are stable and hard to attribute to anyone, because a mandate that stays may have stayed for reasons unrelated to service (good performance, client inertia, the cost of switching). This asymmetry is one reason sales results look more measurable than RM results even when RM work matters as much.
> 
> **Targets.** Where targets exist they are usually set on things the individual can influence only partly. A salesperson can control the number and quality of conversations, the accuracy of the diagnosis and the quality of the proposal. They cannot control the client's timetable, the consultant's rating of the strategy or the investment team's performance record. Good managers therefore watch *leading indicators* (qualified pipeline, meeting quality, win rate on searches entered) alongside the flow number.
> 
> **Negotiation.** Fee negotiation is constrained on three sides: the firm's pricing policy and approval process, the client's benchmark of what similar mandates cost, and, for some clients, "most favoured nation" clauses that promise a client it will not pay more than a comparable client. A coverage professional's judgement shows in knowing what to ask approval for, and in explaining to the client what a lower fee would and would not change.
> 
> **Internal coordination.** The scarce internal resource is specialist time, especially portfolio managers'. Sales competes for it before the client pays anything; RM draws on it to keep a paying client informed. Deciding when a client conversation genuinely needs the portfolio manager is a recurring judgement call for both.
> 
> **Commercial responsibility.** Retained revenue is not the same as incremental revenue. If a client was never going to leave, retaining it added nothing that would not have happened anyway. If a client stayed because a problem was explained honestly and fixed, the RM work was decisive. Firms cannot observe the counterfactual, so credit allocation is partly convention.


## Why "sales wins, RM retains" is only a first approximation

Six situations where the slogan breaks down:

1. **Growth from existing clients.** A scheme with one mandate that awards a second is both retained and new business. Some firms route the second mandate to the original salesperson, some to the relationship manager, some to whoever the client called. The client usually neither knows nor cares.
2. **The consultant channel.** Many UK pension schemes choose managers on advice from an investment consultant. Trustees of occupational schemes are required to set objectives for the firms that give them investment consultancy services. *[Verified]* [W3-S02] If a strategy is not rated by the consultant's research team it may never reach a shortlist, however good the client-facing salesperson is. **Consultant relations** is therefore a third function that both wins and retains, and it reports into different places at different firms.
3. **Small and regional teams.** A team covering a smaller market may have three or four people. Each covers a list of organisations from first meeting to final exit. The labels exist on paper only.
4. **Handover models.** Under a "hunter and farmer" model, sales hands the client to RM after funding. Under a "cradle to grave" model the same person stays. Under a paired model, a salesperson and a relationship manager cover the client together from the pitch onward. Each model changes what the two jobs feel like.
5. **Outsourced chief investment officer (OCIO) and fiduciary management.** Here the product *is* an ongoing relationship: the manager takes delegated responsibility for much of the scheme's investment decision-making. Winning is a formal, regulated process. From 1 October 2022, trustees of UK occupational schemes must run a qualifying competitive tender, inviting bids from at least three unconnected providers, before an appointment that would take fiduciary-managed assets to 20% or more of the scheme's manageable assets. *[Verified]* [W3-S01] After appointment, the "relationship manager" may be a client director with real influence over how the portfolio is explained and governed. The sales and RM skill sets overlap heavily.
6. **Third-party wealth distribution.** When the buyer is a wealth manager's fund selector, there is rarely a single moment of "winning". A fund is researched, approved, made available on platforms, adopted by advisers and then either accumulates assets or does not. Keeping a place on an approved list is continuous selling and continuous servicing at once.


> **[Note: A better mental model]**
>
> Think of two *activities* that every coverage professional performs in different proportions: **origination** (finding and winning a new piece of business) and **stewardship** (looking after business already won). A job title tells you the expected proportion. It does not tell you the actual one.


## How the split varies by firm and team

| Coverage model | How it works | Where it tends to appear | Main trade-off |
|---|---|---|---|
| Separate teams (hunter and farmer) | Sales wins, then hands over to RM at or after funding | Large managers with many institutional clients | Specialisation, at the cost of a handover the client can feel |
| Paired coverage | A salesperson and a relationship manager cover each client together | Large, complex or strategic clients | Continuity, at the cost of two people's time |
| Single owner (cradle to grave) | One person covers a client through its whole life | Small regional teams, boutiques, some wealth channels | Clear accountability, at the cost of less time for prospecting |
| Client director plus servicing team | A senior commercial owner supported by a client service or operations team | OCIO and large segregated mandates | Senior attention, with day-to-day work delegated |
| Channel-based | Coverage organised by intermediary (consultant, platform, private bank) | Wholesale and third-party wealth | Deep channel knowledge, with less direct contact with end investors |

*[Layer B · interpretation]* Which model a given Goldman Sachs team uses is *[Not publicly established]* in this chapter. Chapter 3 records what public sources show team by team.

## Adjacent roles: what this job is not

The roles below are often confused with client coverage in asset management. The table is compact by design; each role is a career in its own right.

| Role | Purpose | Who the client is | What is sold or decided | Decision rights |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Asset management sales and RM (this role) | Match clients' investment needs to the manager's capabilities and keep the relationship sound | Pension schemes, insurers, sovereign funds, endowments, wealth managers | A mandate or fund holding, paid for by an ongoing fee on assets | Recommends and coordinates. Pricing and terms need approval. No investment discretion |
| Investment banking | Advise companies and governments on raising capital and on mergers and acquisitions | Corporate boards, founders, governments, financial sponsors | A transaction, paid for by a one-off fee | Advises; the client's board decides |
| Markets sales and trading | Help clients trade securities and manage risk through the bank's market-making desks | Asset managers, hedge funds, corporates, other banks | A trade or a hedge, paid through spread or commission | Sales advises on execution; traders commit the bank's balance sheet within risk limits |
| Portfolio management | Decide what a fund or mandate owns | The fund's investors, via the mandate | Buy, sell and sizing decisions | Full discretion within the investment guidelines |
| Investment research | Form views on securities, sectors or economies to inform investment decisions | Portfolio managers (buy-side) or external investors (sell-side) | A recommendation | Recommends; does not trade |
| Private wealth advice | Advise individuals and families on their whole financial position | Wealthy individuals, families, their offices | Advice, portfolios and banking services | Advises; sometimes manages on a discretionary basis under an agreed mandate |
| Fund operations | Make sure trades settle, assets are valued and records are right | Internal teams and, indirectly, every investor | Accuracy and control | Operates processes; escalates exceptions |
| Investment consulting | Advise asset owners on strategy and on which managers to hire | Trustees and investment committees | Advice, paid by fee; independent of any one manager | Recommends managers; the trustees decide |
| Product or investment specialist | Represent an investment team to clients in depth | Internal coverage teams and their clients | Explanation of a strategy, not a decision about it | Speaks for the strategy; does not usually trade |
| Client service | Handle reporting, queries and operational requests for existing clients | Existing clients' operations and finance staff | Timely, accurate service | Resolves within procedure; escalates commercial matters to RM |
| RFP team | Write formal responses to tenders and due-diligence questionnaires (DDQs) | Internal sales teams and, through them, prospects | A compliant, accurate written response | Owns the answer library and process; content owners approve the facts |

Three distinctions candidates most often blur:

- **Asset management sales is not markets sales.** Markets sales sells a transaction today and the bank earns a spread on it. Asset management sales proposes that a client delegate money for years and the firm earns a fee for as long as it stays. The time horizon, the product and the conflict-of-interest profile all differ.
- **Asset management RM is not private wealth advice.** A private wealth adviser owes advice to an individual about that individual's whole situation. An institutional relationship manager represents one manager to a professional client that has its own advisers and its own fiduciary duties.
- **A coverage professional is not an investment consultant.** The consultant is paid by the client to be impartial between managers. The coverage professional is paid by one manager. Being honest about that difference is part of being trusted.

## What each level of seniority realistically contributes

*[Layer B · interpretation]* The following is general practice, set out in full in the role cards (`data/roles.json`) with decision rights for each level.

- A **Summer Analyst** produces inputs: research on a client, a first draft of slides, a reconciled data table, notes from a meeting they observed. They do not own a client, quote a fee or send unreviewed material externally.
- An **Analyst** owns recurring inputs end to end: the briefing before every meeting, the pipeline file, the first draft of a review pack, the tracking of actions after a meeting.
- An **Associate** owns pieces of the process: coordinating an RFP response, running a smaller client's review cycle with supervision, checking an analyst's work.
- A **Vice President or Executive Director-level** professional owns client relationships or a territory and carries a commercial number.
- A **Managing Director** who leads a team owns the strategy for the segment, the largest relationships, pricing escalations and hiring.

How those titles map onto each other in a particular office is covered in Chapter 4. Do not assume the ladder is identical in every division or region.

## Five illustrative working days

Each day below is *[Illustrative example]*. They are composites written to show the *kind* of work, in a plausible order. They contain no clock times and no time-share percentages because no public source supports any. Real days are less tidy.


> **[Illustrative example: Institutional sales (a mid-level salesperson covering UK pension schemes)]**
>
> **Start of day.** Reads overnight notes: a consultant has published a search for a buy-and-maintain credit manager on behalf of the fictional Northfield Engineering Pension Scheme. Checks the CRM record. The scheme was met once, two years ago. Asks the analyst for a one-page briefing by midday.
> 
> **Morning.** Call with the consultant-relations colleague: is the firm's strategy rated by this consultant, and who is the field consultant advising Northfield? Then a call with the credit product specialist to test fit. The scheme wants a low-turnover portfolio matched to its benefit payments; one of the firm's two credit strategies fits and the other does not.
> 
> **Middle of the day.** Reviews the analyst's briefing, corrects the description of the scheme's funding position, and decides to enter the search. Kicks off the RFP: confirms deadline, page limit and which questions need fresh input from the portfolio managers.
> 
> **Afternoon.** A first meeting with a different prospect, a university endowment, which ends politely with no interest. Writes it up in four lines and sets a reminder for six months. Then a pitch rehearsal for a final presentation next week, where the portfolio manager's answer on downgrades is too long and gets rewritten.
> 
> **End of day.** Updates the pipeline: one opportunity added, one moved back a stage because the client has delayed its decision to the next trustee meeting.
> 
> *What this day shows:* most of the work is diagnosis and coordination. The single most consequential decision was choosing which strategy fits and declining to offer the one that does not.



> **[Illustrative example: Institutional relationship management (covering six funded pension clients)]**
>
> **Start of day.** The quarterly report for the fictional Westmere County Pension Fund is due to go out. The analyst flags that the benchmark return in the draft differs from last quarter's methodology. Holds the report, asks the performance team to confirm, and tells the client's officer it will arrive a day late and why.
> 
> **Morning.** Preparation call with the portfolio manager before tomorrow's review meeting with a corporate scheme whose mandate has lagged its benchmark for three quarters. Agrees who says what: the relationship manager opens with the numbers plainly stated, the portfolio manager explains the two positions that caused most of the shortfall, and neither predicts a recovery date.
> 
> **Middle of the day.** A client's new head of pensions calls with an unfamiliar question about how the mandate would be affected if the scheme moved towards an insurance buyout. Does not guess. Promises an answer within two days and books time with the solutions team.
> 
> **Afternoon.** Works through a guideline change request: a client wants to add an exclusion list. Coordinates legal, the portfolio manager and compliance so the amendment is feasible and correctly worded before anything is promised.
> 
> **End of day.** Writes minutes and actions from last week's meeting, which should have gone sooner. Sends them with each action owned and dated.
> 
> *What this day shows:* the work is continuous and much of it is preventing small failures from becoming reasons to leave. The hardest moment was choosing accuracy over punctuality on the report.



> **[Illustrative example: Third-party wealth sales (covering UK wealth managers and private banks)]**
>
> **Start of day.** Reviews flow data for the funds on the approved list at the fictional wealth manager Harbour & Vale. One fund is approved but has taken almost no money in six months. Approval did not produce adoption.
> 
> **Morning.** Meeting with a fund selector at a different firm who is reviewing global equity managers. The selector has forty minutes, a scoring template and three competitors to see this week. Leads with the two questions the selector asked last time, then lets the investment specialist cover process.
> 
> **Middle of the day.** Follows up with data the selector requested in a specific format: rolling three-year returns against a named peer group, in the selector's own template. The analyst builds it; it is checked against the official factsheet before sending.
> 
> **Afternoon.** Presents to a room of advisers at Harbour & Vale to explain where the under-used fund fits in their model portfolios. Hears the real obstacle: advisers find the fund hard to explain to clients. Takes that back to marketing as a request for a two-page plain-language guide.
> 
> **End of day.** Logs meetings and updates the list of selectors with open reviews.
> 
> *What this day shows:* the buyer is a professional intermediary with a process, and the end investor is two steps away. Selling continues long after the "sale".



> **[Illustrative example: Third-party wealth relationship management (same channel, existing relationships)]**
>
> **Start of day.** A portfolio manager on a widely held fund has announced a departure. Drafts, with the product team and compliance, a factual note for every firm that holds the fund: what is changing, who takes over, what is not changing, when the client can speak to the new manager.
> 
> **Morning.** Calls the three largest holders personally before the note goes out, so they do not learn it from the trade press.
> 
> **Middle of the day.** A platform has queried a share-class fee discrepancy. Coordinates with operations to confirm which figure is right and sends a corrected data file. For funds held by retail investors, the FCA's Consumer Duty requires firms to "act to deliver good outcomes for retail customers", and manufacturers must ensure their products provide fair value, so pricing data errors are not treated as trivial. *[Verified]* [W3-S04] *[Supported inference]* [W3-S05]
> 
> **Afternoon.** Annual due-diligence meeting with a private bank's research team: updates on the team, process, capacity and operational controls. Much of the meeting is confirming that nothing has changed.
> 
> **End of day.** Updates the watch-list tracker: which holders have put the fund "under review" after the manager change, and when each decision is due.
> 
> *What this day shows:* stewardship in a wealth channel is largely about information reaching many holders accurately and early.



> **[Illustrative example: Consultant relations (covering investment consultants' research teams)]**
>
> **Start of day.** Reads a consultant's quarterly research agenda. Two of the firm's strategies are due for a rating review.
> 
> **Morning.** Prepares, with the investment team, the data pack the consultant's researcher requires: holdings history, team changes, capacity, attribution. The researcher is assessing the strategy for many clients at once, so a weak meeting has wide consequences.
> 
> **Middle of the day.** Accompanies a portfolio manager to the research meeting. Says little. Notes each question the researcher asks twice, because those are the unresolved doubts.
> 
> **Afternoon.** Debriefs sales and RM colleagues: which strategies are rated, which are not, and which searches the firm is therefore unlikely to be invited to. Tells a salesperson that pursuing one prospect is probably wasted effort until the rating changes.
> 
> **End of day.** Sends the researcher the follow-up material promised, exactly as specified.
> 
> *What this day shows:* the "client" is a gatekeeper who never invests a pound directly. Success is measured in ratings and invitations, and the function serves both origination and stewardship.


## Sample work products

These are the things a junior person actually produces. All clients are fictional and all figures *[Illustrative example]*.


> **[Sample work product (fictional): Client briefing (one page, before a first meeting)]**
>
> **Northfield Engineering Pension Scheme: prospect briefing**
> 
> - **What it is.** Closed defined benefit (DB) scheme of a mid-sized engineering company. Assets about £1.2bn per latest published accounts. Source: scheme annual report, page reference noted.
> - **Governance.** Nine trustees including an independent chair. Investment sub-committee meets quarterly. Investment consultant named in the report.
> - **Position.** Funding level improved over three years. Statement of Investment Principles says the scheme is reducing return-seeking assets.
