- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Identify which gatekeepers have a mandate or model that can hold active ETFs, and whether the ETF is on the platforms their advisers use.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** For clients already in the mutual fund, explain the differences honestly (cost, dealing, tax reporting, spread) and handle any switch so the client is not surprised by trading costs.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Cannibalisation of higher-fee share classes; UK platform support for ETFs (fractional dealing, trading charges) is uneven; a low TER leaves little margin; low tracking error means outperformance, if any, is small.
- **A question to ask a client:** Where an active ETF and a fund share class of the same strategy both exist, which would your portfolio process choose, and what on your platform decides that?
- **Indicators to watch:** European active ETF assets and flows; number of GS UCITS ETFs and their assets; whether UK platforms add ETF model portfolio functionality; TER moves by competitors.
- **Sources:** R4-S01, R4-S17, R4-S38

### Evergreen private-markets funds and ELTIF 2.0 reach the wealth channel `D-R4-02`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
  - Switzerland
  - Benelux
  - Nordics
  - Middle East
- **Event date:** 2023-07 to 2026-03
- **Publication date:** 2026-03-11
- **1 · What happened:** Goldman Sachs Alternatives built a 'G-Series' of open-ended private-markets funds for qualified investors (G-PE launched 2 April 2025) and on 11 March 2026 added an ELTIF range starting with the G-European Credit ELTIF, its first evergreen product under ELTIF 2.0. G-Series funds are sold through GS Private Wealth Management and select third-party distributors.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Private banks and wealth managers can offer private equity, private credit and infrastructure to eligible individuals with periodic subscriptions and limited redemptions instead of ten-year drawdown funds.
- **3 · What decision changes:** The distributor must decide whether clients are eligible and suitable, how large an illiquid sleeve can be, and whether its systems and advisers can handle periodic dealing and redemption limits.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Clients may fund the allocation from listed equity or bond funds, some of which may be the same manager's.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: pair the product with adviser education and clear liquidity terms; work through a dedicated alternatives-for-wealth specialist team alongside the TPW coverage person.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Find distributors with an approved private-markets allocation and the operational set-up to deal in semi-liquid funds; expect a long due-diligence process.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Keep advisers informed on valuation, redemption queue and gating terms before stress arrives, not after.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Liquidity mismatch if many investors redeem together; valuation opacity; higher fees; suitability risk under the Consumer Duty; UK retail access differs from the EEA (ELTIF is an EEA label).
- **A question to ask a client:** What share of a typical client portfolio could you put in assets that can only be redeemed quarterly, and who in your firm signs that off?
- **Indicators to watch:** G-Series assets if disclosed; redemption requests across the semi-liquid sector; FCA and ESMA statements on semi-liquid funds; number of distributors named in GS releases.
- **Sources:** R4-S18, R4-S19, R4-S20, R4-S21

### UK product disclosure is rewritten: Consumer Composite Investments regime `D-R4-03`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2025-12-08
- **Publication date:** 2025-12-08
- **1 · What happened:** The FCA published final CCI rules (PS25/20) on 8 December 2025. Firms can opt in from 6 April 2026 and the regime is fully in force from 8 June 2027. It applies to manufacturers and distributors.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Advisers, platforms and investors will receive a redesigned product summary with a standardised core covering costs, risk and past performance.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Distributors must decide when to switch their systems to the new summaries and how they will present comparable cost figures across managers.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** No change to holdings by itself, but clearer or differently calculated cost figures can change how an existing fund looks against peers.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: produce the new summaries early for the fund range sold in the UK and give distributors the data feeds they need.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Being operationally ready is a hygiene factor in due diligence; lateness can keep a fund off a platform.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Explain to existing distributors what changes in the documents their advisers hand to clients.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Mostly an operational change; unlikely to win business; cross-border ranges must run UK and EU disclosure regimes side by side.
- **A question to ask a client:** How are you planning the move to the new product summaries, and what do you need from fund managers before June 2027?
- **Indicators to watch:** FCA implementation updates; distributor data-template standards; how overseas (OFR) funds are treated.
- **Sources:** R4-S28

### Targeted support: a new way for UK firms to suggest investments without full advice `D-R4-04`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2025-12
- **Publication date:** 2025-12
- **1 · What happened:** The FCA's PS25/22 (December 2025) set near-final rules for 'targeted support', where firms give ready-made suggestions to groups of consumers with common characteristics. The FCA expected the rules to take effect from 6 April 2026.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Platforms, banks and pension providers can point non-advised customers towards specific investment solutions, which may bring cash savers into funds.
- **3 · What decision changes:** A distributor decides whether to offer targeted support and which funds or portfolios sit behind each ready-made suggestion.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Limited direct effect; over time default-style multi-asset solutions may gather a larger share of new non-advised money.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: offer simple, low-cost building blocks or multi-asset funds that a distributor can defend as a ready-made suggestion under the Consumer Duty.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Ask distributors whether they will launch targeted support and how the underlying funds will be chosen.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Support distributors with the evidence they need to show the suggested product offers fair value for the segment.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Distributors may use in-house or index funds; take-up may be slow; the FCA says consumers remain responsible for their decisions, so reputational risk is real if outcomes disappoint. Commencement on 6 April 2026 was not confirmed in this research session.
- **A question to ask a client:** Are you planning a targeted support service, and if so how will you select what sits behind each suggestion?
- **Indicators to watch:** Which firms get the new permission; FCA data on take-up; whether suggestions favour in-house, passive or third-party funds.
- **Sources:** R4-S29

### Overseas Funds Regime decides how EEA-domiciled funds stay on sale in the UK `D-R4-05`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2024-07
- **Publication date:** 2024-07
- **1 · What happened:** The FCA's PS24/7 (July 2024) implemented the Overseas Funds Regime. EEA UCITS, except money-market funds, can apply for recognition to be marketed to UK retail investors, replacing the Temporary Marketing Permissions Regime.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** UK wealth managers and platforms can keep using Luxembourg and Irish UCITS and UCITS ETFs once recognised.
- **3 · What decision changes:** A fund selector needs to know each overseas fund has recognition; a manager must decide which funds are worth registering.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Funds not moved into the OFR can no longer be promoted to UK retail investors, which can force switches.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: ensure the cross-border range sold in the UK is recognised on time and tell distributors early about any fund that will not be.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Recognition status is a due-diligence item for any new UK platform listing.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Proactively confirm the status of each fund a client holds.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Low-probability, high-impact operational risk; money-market funds are excluded from the first equivalence decision; the TMPR end date after any extension was not verified here.
- **A question to ask a client:** Do any funds on your list face an OFR recognition question, and how are you handling them?
- **Indicators to watch:** FCA landing-slot timetable; any equivalence decision for money-market funds; UK-EU regulatory divergence.
- **Sources:** R4-S30

### Value-for-money scrutiny: Consumer Duty and annual assessments of value `D-R4-06`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2019-09 onward
- **Publication date:** 2026-10-23
- **1 · What happened:** UK authorised fund managers have had to assess each fund's value at least annually since September 2019, and the Consumer Duty adds a 'price and value' outcome that distributors must also meet.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Wealth managers must be able to show that the total cost a client pays, including the fund, is fair for what the client receives.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Fund selectors weigh fees more heavily and may remove funds whose cost they cannot justify, or switch to cheaper share classes, ETFs or index funds.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Higher-fee share classes face pressure to reprice or be replaced.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: offer institutional-priced share classes for scale, be explicit about what the active fee buys, and supply value evidence the distributor can file.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** A credible answer on cost is needed before investment merits are heard.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Help existing distributors document value each year; raise share-class conversions before the client asks.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Fee cuts reduce revenue on assets already held; cheapest is not always best value; a manager's own value assessment is not independent evidence.
- **A question to ask a client:** When you last reviewed value across your buy list, what made a fund fail?
- **Indicators to watch:** FCA Consumer Duty reviews; published assessment-of-value outcomes; ETF TERs (GS active ETFs at 0.20%-0.25% are data points).
- **Sources:** R4-S31, R4-S32, R4-S01

### Fund tokenisation rules finalised in the UK `D-R4-07`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
  - Liquidity Solutions
- **Event date:** 2026-04-30
- **Publication date:** 2026-04-30
- **1 · What happened:** The FCA published PS26/7 on 30 April 2026 with guidance for running fund registers on distributed ledger technology and optional rules for direct-to-fund dealing, effective immediately.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Platforms and wealth managers may in time deal in fund units with faster settlement and fewer intermediaries.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Distributors decide whether their systems will support tokenised share classes; managers decide whether to launch them.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** None immediately; tokenised share classes would sit alongside existing ones.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: pilot tokenised share classes where a distributor asks for them, most plausibly in money-market funds first.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Mostly a watching brief; relevant in conversations with platforms and digital wealth firms.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Explain what, if anything, changes for a client holding the conventional share class.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Demand is unproven; operational and legal risk; benefits accrue mainly to the back office. Adoption levels were not researched in this stream.
- **A question to ask a client:** Is tokenised fund dealing on your technology roadmap, or is it too early?
- **Indicators to watch:** Launches of tokenised UK authorised funds; platform announcements; FCA follow-up work.
- **Sources:** R4-S34

### Model portfolio services concentrate buying power in fewer hands `D-R4-08`

- **Evidence status:** Supported inference
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** Not publicly disclosed
- **Publication date:** Not publicly disclosed
- **1 · What happened:** A wealth manager's commentary reports the UK model portfolio service (MPS) market at about £190bn, up 32% year on year. More adviser money is run through centrally built model portfolios.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Advisers outsource fund selection to a discretionary manager's model, so one investment team's decision moves money for thousands of end clients.
- **3 · What decision changes:** The decision moves from many individual advisers to a small number of MPS investment committees with tight cost budgets.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Funds held adviser-by-adviser can be sold when the client is moved into a model that does not hold them.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: cover MPS providers as institutional-style buyers, with low-cost building blocks, capacity and consistent data.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** A single model inclusion can be worth more than many adviser meetings, but the bar on fees and due diligence is higher.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Inclusion can be reversed at any rebalance, so regular portfolio-manager access and honest performance explanation matter.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** The £190bn figure was seen only in a search result and its original source is unknown; MPS cost caps favour passive funds; concentration cuts both ways when a model drops a fund.
- **A question to ask a client:** How much of your firm's new money now goes through models, and what cost budget does the model team work to?
- **Indicators to watch:** Primary MPS market data (find and cite the original provider); share of passive in MPS; platform MPS charges.
- **Sources:** R4-S37

### Consolidation and outside capital in UK advice and wealth firms `D-R4-09`

- **Evidence status:** Supported inference
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2026-01-28
- **Publication date:** 2026-01-28
- **1 · What happened:** On 28 January 2026 Goldman Sachs Alternatives agreed to take a minority stake in The Private Office, a UK financial planning firm with over £3bn of client funds, subject to FCA approval. It is one public example of institutional capital buying into UK advice firms.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** As advice firms merge or take private-equity backing they tend to centralise investment propositions and reduce the number of funds and managers used.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Fund selection moves to a central investment committee; acquired firms' legacy fund holdings are reviewed.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** A fund held by an acquired firm can be removed when its clients are moved to the acquirer's central proposition.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: build relationships with consolidators' central investment teams early and offer scale pricing.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Map who decides after each deal; the adviser relationship that won the business may no longer control it.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Watch for ownership changes at existing clients and re-earn the position with the new decision-makers.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** The TPO deal is an investment by the Alternatives business and the release does not link it to fund distribution; wider consolidation data was not sourced in this session; a manager owning a stake in an adviser raises conflict-of-interest questions.
- **A question to ask a client:** After your recent acquisitions, how are you bringing investment propositions together, and who now owns fund selection?
- **Indicators to watch:** Deal announcements; FCA commentary on consolidators; number of funds on consolidators' central lists.
- **Sources:** R4-S23

### Government push for a UK retail investment culture `D-R4-10`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2025-11-26
- **Publication date:** 2025-11-26
- **1 · What happened:** Budget 2025 (26 November 2025) announced reforms to ISAs as part of a wider strategy to develop a retail investment culture.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** More savers may be steered from cash into investments, mostly through platforms and banks.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Platforms and wealth managers decide what default or suggested investments to put in front of new investors.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Little direct effect on existing fund holdings.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Layer B: simple multi-asset and core equity funds or ETFs that distributors can use for first-time investors.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Relevant to platform and bank distributors more than to private banks.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Not a relationship issue unless a distributor redesigns its ISA range.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** The specific ISA limits and dates were not read in the portions of the Budget document fetched, so do not quote numbers from this entry; behavioural response is uncertain; new money may go mainly to index funds.
- **A question to ask a client:** Do you expect ISA changes to alter what your non-advised customers buy?
- **Indicators to watch:** Final ISA rules and dates; platform flow data; any later Budget changes before the cutoff.
- **Sources:** R4-S35

### Leadership change in GSAM's EMEA Third Party Wealth business `D-R4-11`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2026-04
- **Publication date:** 2026-04-21
- **1 · What happened:** Hilary Lopez, head of the EMEA Third Party Wealth business since 2021, left in spring 2026 and is reported to be joining BlackRock as head of European wealth. Trade press says the global TPW business continues under co-heads Padi Raphael and Bryon Lake. No EMEA successor was found.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Distributors' senior contact changes; day-to-day coverage usually continues through country teams.
- **3 · What decision changes:** None directly; some distributors review a manager's commitment to the channel when leadership changes.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** None directly.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** Not publicly established.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** For a candidate: know who publicly leads the channel globally and do not name an EMEA head without checking for a newer announcement.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Shows why relationship knowledge has to sit with the institution, not one person.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Destination reported by trade press, not confirmed by either firm in sources read; a successor may have been announced after the searches run here.
- **A question to ask a client:** Not a client question.
- **Indicators to watch:** Any GSAM announcement naming a new EMEA TPW head; changes to country heads.
- **Sources:** R4-S02, R4-S03, R4-S04

### Dutch pension funds move from defined benefit to the new contribution-based system (Wtp) `D-R5-01`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Benelux
  - Insurance
- **Event date:** 1 January 2026 (largest wave so far); deadline 1 January 2028
- **Publication date:** 2026-04-28
- **1 · What happened:** Thirty Dutch pension funds, holding about EUR 542bn, had converted to the new system by 31 March 2026; 24 of them moved on 1 January 2026. Remaining funds must transfer accrued entitlements by 1 January 2028.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** A fund that used to promise a fixed pension now manages personal pots by age group. Younger members can take more risk and need less interest-rate protection; older members still need stable income. The fund's hedging and return portfolios have to be redesigned around age cohorts and a solidarity reserve.
- **3 · What decision changes:** How much interest-rate hedging to keep and for which cohorts; what goes into the return portfolio (more equities, credit, private assets); how the reserve is invested; whether the fiduciary manager and mandate structure still fit.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Long-dated swap and bond hedges can shrink for younger cohorts; existing matching mandates may be resized or re-benchmarked; reporting moves to cohort level and becomes more frequent.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Offer cohort-aware fixed income and return-seeking building blocks, and analysis specific to each fund's lifecycle design, rather than a generic LDI pitch. GSAM's Dutch presence since the 2022 NN IP acquisition is relevant capacity, but no public source ties a specific Wtp mandate to GSAM.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** For funds still to convert (2027 and 2028 waves), the opening is the portfolio redesign: asking what the new return portfolio needs that the old one lacked.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** For existing mandates, the job is to keep the mandate fit for purpose through conversion: revised guidelines, benchmarks and reporting, and honesty if a matching mandate should shrink.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** The January 2026 wave passed calmly, so the 'forced selling' story may be overdone; estimates of future hedge reduction come from interested parties and carry wide uncertainty. Some funds keep higher hedging than the market expected. Many Dutch funds delegate to fiduciary managers, so the real buyer may be the fiduciary, not the fund.
- **A question to ask a client:** Which of your age cohorts will hold less interest-rate hedging after conversion, and what will replace it in their return portfolio?
- **Indicators to watch:** DNB quarterly counts of converted funds and assets; conversion dates of the largest remaining funds; long-end euro swap spreads around 1 January 2027 and 2028; insurer demand for long duration ahead of January 2027 Solvency II changes.
- **Sources:** R5-S35, R5-S36, R5-S37

### UK and EU money market fund rules: higher expected liquidity, no forced fees or gates `D-R5-02`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Liquidity Solutions
  - UK Institutional
  - Insurance
- **Event date:** 8 June 2026 (FCA update); UK regime targeted end-2026
- **Publication date:** 2026-06-08
- **1 · What happened:** The FCA said the Government expects to repeal the UK Money Market Funds Regulation by end-2026, with new FCA rules. Guidance will expect stable-NAV funds to hold 40% weekly liquid assets (20% for variable-NAV) and the link between liquidity thresholds and fees or gates will be removed. A European Commission report in 2026 pointed to the same 40%/20% levels as supervisory benchmarks without rewriting the EU regulation. EU-domiciled funds, about 90% of sterling MMF assets, stay on sale in the UK under an extended temporary regime pending an equivalence decision.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** A treasurer using a stable-value fund gets a fund that holds more cash-like assets and is less likely to gate in a stress, at the cost of slightly lower yield. Continued UK access to EU funds is expected but not yet permanent.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Whether to keep operating cash in LVNAV funds, move part to government-only (Public Debt CNAV) funds, or segment cash into operating, reserve and strategic buckets with short-duration strategies for the last.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Existing MMF holdings are unaffected day to day; yield may drift a little lower as funds hold more weekly liquidity; investment policies that reference old thresholds or 'fees and gates' triggers need updating.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Explain how each GS liquidity fund already sits against the new expectations, and offer cash segmentation: money market funds for daily cash, short-duration accounts for cash not needed for months.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Lead with resilience and reporting (for example through the Mosaic portal) rather than yield alone; most treasurers already hold several providers, so the realistic aim is a larger share of balances.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Review each client's investment policy against the new rules before the client asks, and flag any share class or domicile changes early.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Final FCA rules were not published at the cutoff, and the equivalence decision could attach conditions. Balances are driven far more by interest rates and bank deposit pricing than by regulation. Fees are thin, so a small yield difference moves large balances quickly in either direction.
- **A question to ask a client:** Does your treasury policy still refer to liquidity fees and redemption gates as triggers, and how would you want your cash split if stable-value funds yield a little less?
- **Indicators to watch:** FCA policy statement and interim guidance on weekly liquid assets; HM Treasury legislation; equivalence decision for EU MMFs (indicated for 2027); central bank rate path; industry sterling MMF assets.
- **Sources:** R5-S32, R5-S33, R5-S34, R5-S01

### Tokenised money market fund shares: collateral use, not yet a new product for treasurers `D-R5-03`

- **Evidence status:** Supported inference
- **Teams:** 
  - Liquidity Solutions
- **Event date:** 23 July 2025
- **Publication date:** 2025-07-23
- **1 · What happened:** BNY and Goldman Sachs launched a solution that records 'mirror' tokens of money market fund shares on Goldman's GS DAP ledger, with subscriptions and redemptions through BNY's LiquidityDirect and BNY remaining the official record. GSAM was among the first fund managers.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** In principle an institution could post fund shares as collateral directly instead of redeeming to cash first, keeping yield and avoiding a forced sale in stress.
- **3 · What decision changes:** For now, little: it is an operational option for institutions that post margin, not a different fund.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** No change to existing fund holdings.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Be able to explain plainly what tokenisation changes (transfer and collateral use) and what it does not (the fund's assets, risk and regulation).
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** A conversation opener with derivatives users and hedge funds that post margin daily; not a reason by itself to switch provider.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Keep clients informed without overselling; note any availability limits by fund domicile.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Launch coverage was US-focused; no public evidence was found on volumes or on availability for European funds or EMEA clients. Regulators in Europe have not broadly accepted tokenised fund shares as margin.
- **A question to ask a client:** How much cash do you redeem from money market funds each month just to meet margin calls?
- **Indicators to watch:** Regulatory acceptance of tokenised MMF shares as collateral in the UK and EU; extension to European-domiciled funds; adoption figures.
- **Sources:** R5-S40, R5-S33

### Insurance capital rules change in 2027 (EU) and the UK loosens matching-adjustment investing `D-R5-04`

- **Evidence status:** Supported inference
- **Teams:** 
  - Insurance
  - Benelux
  - Nordics
- **Event date:** EU: 30 January 2027; UK consultation 8 April 2025
- **Publication date:** 2025-04-08
- **1 · What happened:** Amended EU Solvency II rules (Directive (EU) 2025/2) take effect on 30 January 2027. In the UK, the PRA consulted in April 2025 on a Matching Adjustment Investment Accelerator that lets insurers add self-assessed eligible assets to matching-adjustment portfolios before formal approval, within the lower of 5% of liabilities or GBP 2bn and a 24-month regularisation window.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** An insurer chooses assets by yield after capital charge, not by yield alone. Rule changes alter which assets are 'capital-efficient' and how quickly an annuity writer can invest in new private asset types.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Allocation to long-dated and private credit assets; how much to outsource; which managers can structure assets to be matching-adjustment eligible.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Existing portfolios are re-tested against new capital charges; some long-duration and equity positions become cheaper or dearer to hold.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Show the capital-adjusted return of proposed assets under the client's own regulatory basis; GSAM describes 'capital-efficient investment strategies' and asset-liability advice as part of its insurance offer.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Sales must speak the insurer's language (capital charge, book yield, liability matching) before talking about products.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Relationship managers coordinate regulatory reporting data and help the client evidence eligibility of assets already held.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** The final UK policy statement and the detailed EU changes were not read in this research, so details must be checked before quoting. Regulators are also wary of private-asset concentration at annuity writers. Survey intentions to raise private assets may not turn into allocations.
- **A question to ask a client:** Which assets in your portfolio become more or less capital-efficient under the January 2027 rules?
- **Indicators to watch:** PRA policy statement on the accelerator; EIOPA and Commission delegated acts before January 2027; insurers' reported private-asset allocations.
- **Sources:** R5-S38, R5-S39, R5-S02

### Insurers say they will keep adding private assets (GSAM Global Insurance Survey 2026) `D-R5-05`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Insurance
- **Event date:** Fieldwork 11 January-3 February 2026
- **Publication date:** 2026-03-25
- **1 · What happened:** GSAM's 15th annual survey of 434 insurance CIOs and CFOs found 62% plan to increase private asset allocations in 2026; asset-backed finance had the highest net intended increase (38%), then investment-grade private placements (35%) and senior direct lending (33%). 55% expect a US recession within three years, up from 46%.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Insurers are looking for extra yield from illiquid, often investment-grade, private credit while worrying about the cycle and valuations.
- **3 · What decision changes:** How much liquidity to give up, and whether to build private credit capability in-house or outsource.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Public investment-grade bond portfolios may be trimmed to fund private placements; liquidity buffers need re-checking.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Private credit and asset-backed finance origination offered inside an insurance mandate with capital and liquidity analysis attached.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** The survey is itself a sales and credibility tool: it gives a reason to open a conversation with a CIO using peer data.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Use the peer data in portfolio reviews to test, not to push, the client's current allocation.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** It is a survey run by a manager that sells these assets, and it measures intentions. No EMEA breakdown was found on the pages read. Rising recession expectations cut against adding illiquid credit.
- **A question to ask a client:** Where does your private-asset allocation sit against your liquidity stress test, not against your peers?
- **Indicators to watch:** Next survey (around March 2027); regulator statements on private assets at insurers; default and valuation data in private credit.
- **Sources:** R5-S02, R5-S03

### Goldman Sachs builds onshore presence in the Gulf: Abu Dhabi, Kuwait, Riyadh regional headquarters `D-R5-06`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Middle East
- **Event date:** 2023 to December 2025
- **Publication date:** 2025-12-11
- **1 · What happened:** Goldman Sachs announced an Abu Dhabi Global Market office in 2023, opened a Kuwait office in October 2025 and officially opened a new office and regional headquarters in Riyadh's King Abdullah Financial District in December 2025, adding to Dubai, Doha and Riyadh (since 2008). Asset and wealth management is named among its Saudi businesses.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Gulf sovereign and public institutions increasingly expect managers to be present, licensed and hiring locally, and to offer partnerships and co-investment rather than off-the-shelf funds.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Which managers receive large strategic mandates and partnerships, and whether coverage is run from the region or from London.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Existing relationships may be re-papered with local entities; expectations for local servicing and knowledge transfer rise.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Coverage closer to clients and bespoke structures such as the US$1bn Mubadala private credit separately managed account (2024), which was run by Goldman Sachs Alternatives.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** A small number of very large, sophisticated clients: the sale is a multi-year institutional partnership involving senior people across the firm, not a product pitch.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Servicing involves co-investment reporting, training and secondment-type requests as much as performance reviews.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** The office openings are firm-wide; public sources do not say which house asset-management client coverage. A 2027 Dubai CSG student role is listed, so not all Middle East coverage sits in London. Concentration risk: a handful of clients, whose priorities can shift with oil revenue and domestic spending plans.
- **A question to ask a client:** How do you weigh a manager's local presence against its global investment platform when you award a strategic mandate?
- **Indicators to watch:** Further local licences and hires; announced sovereign partnerships; oil price and Gulf fiscal balances; domestic-investment mandates of sovereign funds.
- **Sources:** R5-S04, R5-S28, R5-S29, R5-S30, R5-S31, R5-S18

### Policy rates are rising again: Bank Rate up to 4% in November 2026 `D-R8-01`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - All
  - UK Institutional
  - OCIO
  - Insurance
  - Liquidity Solutions
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2026-11-05
- **Publication date:** 2026-11-05
- **1 · What happened:** The Bank of England raised Bank Rate by 0.25pp to 4% (8-1; one member wanted 4.25%), its first increase since 2023, with CPI at 3.5% and projected near 5% by mid-2027. The Fed held at 3.75-4.00% on 28 Oct with two dissents for a hike; the ECB held its deposit rate at 2.50% after a September increase.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Higher yields lower the present value of DB liabilities and of existing bonds; cash and money market yields rise; leveraged borrowers and floating-rate private credit face higher servicing costs; inflation-linked liabilities grow.
- **3 · What decision changes:** DB trustees: hedge ratios, collateral buffers, timing of buyout. Treasurers: how much to hold in MMFs versus term. Insurers: reinvestment yields versus credit risk. Wealth selectors: duration and cash allocations.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Mark-to-market losses on long-dated bonds; collateral calls on leveraged LDI (buffers are larger than in 2022); liquidity balances may grow.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Explain funding-level and collateral effects scheme by scheme; show liquidity and short-duration options; avoid presenting a rate view as certainty.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** A reason to open a conversation about liquidity, inflation protection or collateral resilience, not proof that a product should be bought.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Proactive calls to existing clients on what the move did to their portfolio and hedges, before they ask.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** The inflation projection may prove wrong and policy could reverse; schemes that are well hedged may need to do nothing; higher cash yields compete with every fee-bearing product.
- **A question to ask a client:** How did your funding level and collateral headroom move this month, and has that changed your timetable or your liquidity needs?
- **Indicators to watch:** 17 December 2026 MPC; gilt yields and index-linked breakevens; CPI prints; any LDI collateral stress reports.
- **Sources:** R8-S49, R8-S50, R8-S51

### Nuveen completes £9.9bn acquisition of Schroders `D-R8-02`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - UK Institutional
  - OCIO
  - Third Party Wealth
  - Insurance
- **Event date:** 2026-10-01
- **Publication date:** 2026-10-05
- **1 · What happened:** Nuveen, TIAA's asset manager, completed its acquisition of Schroders plc (announced 12 Feb 2026). The combined firm reports about $2.6tn; Schroders operates separately within Nuveen for 12-18 months; London is the non-US headquarters.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Clients of Schroders (including Schroders Solutions' fiduciary and LDI clients and wealth fund buyers) face a change of ownership and, later, possible team, product and system integration.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Trustees and fund selectors decide whether to review the mandate now, add a watch flag, or wait; consultants update manager ratings.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Mandates continue under existing contracts; change-of-control and key-person provisions may be reviewed.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Be ready with accurate, non-disparaging answers if a client or consultant launches a review; do not approach clients with speculation about a competitor.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** A review, if it happens, is initiated by the client or consultant. The sales task is to be shortlisted on merit.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Existing Goldman clients may ask whether Goldman itself is stable; show continuity of people and process.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Most clients do not move on ownership change alone; integration may improve Schroders' offer; Schroders reported positive client feedback; Goldman is also an acquirer (Innovator, NEOS, LCN) and can be asked the same question.
- **A question to ask a client:** When a manager you use changes owner, what would you need to see over the next year to stay comfortable?
- **Indicators to watch:** Consultant rating changes; departures from Schroders Solutions; UK fiduciary retender announcements in 2027.
- **Sources:** R8-S08, R8-S07, R8-S09, R8-S10

### Asset-management consolidation accelerates in 2026 `D-R8-03`

- **Evidence status:** Supported inference
- **Teams:** 
  - All
- **Event date:** 2026
- **Publication date:** 2026-08
- **1 · What happened:** Dealogic counted about $53.8bn of asset-management M&A in the year to late August 2026, including Nuveen-Schroders, a take-private of Janus Henderson, Victory Capital-First Eagle and Wellington-Hartford Funds; BNP Paribas integrated AXA IM; Goldman itself bought Innovator and agreed to buy NEOS and LCN.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Fewer, larger managers; more frequent ownership changes for the funds and mandates clients hold; buyers of scale gain pricing power in passive but margins stay near 30%.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Whether to concentrate with large multi-capability firms or deliberately keep specialists; how often to review managers after ownership changes.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Fund mergers, share-class changes and team turnover at acquired firms.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Present breadth honestly, including where a specialist is the better answer.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Scale and stability arguments carry more weight with small investment teams.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Reassure on continuity; flag early any change that touches the client's mandate.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Scale has not improved industry margins (BCG); integration often disrupts service; clients may respond by diversifying away from the largest firms.
- **A question to ask a client:** Do you want fewer, deeper manager relationships, or do you see concentration with a few large firms as a risk?
- **Indicators to watch:** Further deals; consultant commentary on integration; regulatory review of concentration.
- **Sources:** R8-S54, R8-S08, R8-S20, R8-S25, R8-S48, R8-S38

### Fee compression continues while revenue depends on markets `D-R8-04`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - All
- **Event date:** 2026
- **Publication date:** 2026-04-28
- **1 · What happened:** BCG: institutional fees have fallen about 3% a year; more than 80% of 2025 revenue growth came from market appreciation; costs grew faster than revenue from 2010 to 2025. Morningstar: US asset-weighted fund fees fell to 0.32% in 2025. IC Select: UK fiduciary fees fell 27% over five years.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Clients pay less per pound managed and expect more service; they can credibly threaten to move to a cheaper provider.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Fee renegotiation at review; move from active to passive or active ETF; consolidation of mandates to reach breakpoints.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Existing mandates face repricing requests even when performance is fine.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Defend price with evidence of value, offer breakpoints or broader relationships, and accept that sometimes the cheaper product is the right answer.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Price is rarely the opening argument but must be competitive to reach the shortlist.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Fee conversations are an RM responsibility: prepare the value-for-money case before the client asks.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Fees are observable and value is not, so pressure is structural; for small UK schemes fiduciary fees have actually risen, so the trend is not uniform.
- **A question to ask a client:** When you last reviewed what you pay us, what did you compare it with, and what would you need to see to judge it good value?
- **Indicators to watch:** Morningstar and BCG annual fee studies; UK value-for-money framework for DC; consultant fee surveys.
- **Sources:** R8-S38, R8-S37, R8-S10

### AI moves into RFPs, meeting notes and reporting in distribution teams `D-R8-05`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - All
- **Event date:** 2026-02-19
- **Publication date:** 2026-02-19
- **1 · What happened:** Cerulli: 81% of institutional sales and service teams use AI for RFP content and 77% for meeting notes; far fewer for prospecting or sales strategy. Mercer: 73% of managers use AI for operational efficiency; only 5% let it act autonomously. Isio: 85% of UK fiduciary managers use AI, mainly to draft reports and emails.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Clients may receive faster, more consistent responses; they also ask how AI is governed and whether outputs are checked.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Due-diligence questionnaires now include AI governance; clients may shorten response deadlines.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** No direct portfolio effect.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Use the time saved for accuracy checks and client-specific analysis; be able to explain controls.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Faster proposal turnaround is now expected, so it no longer differentiates on its own.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Automated reporting raises the bar for the human explanation that accompanies it.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Survey figures are self-reported and partly vendor-sponsored; errors in AI-drafted answers create liability; no evidence yet on junior hiring effects; BCG's 25-35% cost-saving figure is an estimate.
- **A question to ask a client:** What do you want to know about how we use AI in the material we send you, and where would you want a named person accountable?
- **Indicators to watch:** Regulatory guidance on AI in client communications; Cerulli/Mercer follow-up surveys; reported headcount changes in distribution.
- **Sources:** R8-S41, R8-S42, R8-S10, R8-S38, R8-S43

### Private credit under strain: elevated redemptions in retail semi-liquid funds `D-R8-06`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
  - Insurance
  - UK Institutional
  - Middle East
  - All
- **Event date:** 2026-07
- **Publication date:** 2026-07
- **1 · What happened:** The Bank of England's July 2026 Financial Stability Report said risky credit markets including private credit remain vulnerable to tighter financing conditions and that redemption requests have been elevated in several retail funds, with some limiting redemptions. Blackstone reported BCRED redemption requests stayed elevated in Q3 2026 even as private-wealth AUM reached $330bn.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Wealth clients in semi-liquid funds may be unable to redeem on demand; insurers and pension funds reassess valuation and liquidity assumptions; rising rates add pressure on borrowers.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Fund selectors tighten due diligence on liquidity terms, valuation policy and leverage; some pause new allocations; insurers review capital treatment.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Holders of evergreen private credit funds face queues or gates; marks may lag.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Lead with liquidity terms and downside cases; do not sell semi-liquid as liquid; be explicit about who the product does not suit.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Selling private markets to wealth now requires proving the client understands redemption limits.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Tell holders early how redemption queues work and what the fund has done; this is where trust is tested.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Stress is concentrated in particular funds and borrowers; private wealth inflows continue (Blackstone BXPE record quarter); the BoE judges households and corporates resilient in aggregate.
- **A question to ask a client:** If you could not redeem for four quarters, would this allocation still be the right size for your clients?
- **Indicators to watch:** BoE December 2026 FSR; redemption data from large evergreen funds; default rates; FCA/ESMA action on semi-liquid funds.
- **Sources:** R8-S52, R8-S34, R8-S53

### UK fiduciary management: fewer mandates, larger ones, falling fees at the top `D-R8-07`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - OCIO
  - UK Institutional
- **Event date:** 2026-04
- **Publication date:** 2026-04
- **1 · What happened:** UK fiduciary assets are above £250bn (+58% in three years) but the number of mandates fell 4% in the year to June 2025, the first fall since 2008, as schemes buy out. OCIO-style mandates are 6% by number and 53% of DB fiduciary AUM. Fees above £500m are falling; below £50m they rose. Providers are consolidating (Mercer-Cardano, Nuveen-Schroders) and Columbia Threadneedle exited.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Large schemes have many bidders and pricing power; small schemes have fewer bidders. About 26% of fully delegated schemes expect buyout within three years, which ends the mandate.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Whether to delegate fully, partially or not at all; whether to run on or buy out; whether to pay for independent oversight (only one in three do).
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Existing fiduciary mandates shrink or terminate at buyout: a good client outcome that removes fee revenue.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Compete where bespoke capability matters (£1bn+); be clear about the endgame path and what happens to fees when the scheme buys out.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Few, very large, slow competitive tenders run by third-party evaluators; losing after months of work is normal.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Help the scheme reach its endgame even though success ends the relationship; propose oversight rather than resist it.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Goldman's UK share is not public; consultant-heritage providers hold about half of mandates by number; run-on with surplus release could slow buyouts (scenario).
- **A question to ask a client:** Is your endgame buyout or run-on, and how should your fiduciary manager be paid in a way that does not reward delaying it?
- **Indicators to watch:** Isio, IC Select, XPS and Quantum annual surveys; large mandate announcements; Pension Schemes Act surplus rules (see R3).
- **Sources:** R8-S10, R8-S09, R8-S11, R8-S26

### Public-private alliances and active ETFs reshape products for wealth intermediaries `D-R8-08`

- **Evidence status:** Supported inference
- **Teams:** 
  - Third Party Wealth
- **Event date:** 2026-07-22
- **Publication date:** 2026-07-22
- **1 · What happened:** Wellington, Vanguard and Blackstone launched two public-private funds (US, July 2026); Capital Group and KKR took a public-private credit fund to Europe and Asia-Pacific via HSBC Private Bank (May 2026); Goldman agreed a T. Rowe Price partnership (Sept 2025). European active ETF assets reached about EUR 117bn at end Q3 2026, with J.P. Morgan holding about 41%; passive ETFs took about 57% of Q3 2026 European fund flows.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Fund selectors face more wrappers for similar exposures and must judge liquidity and layered fees; shelf space goes to firms with distribution access.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Which vehicle (mutual fund, active ETF, evergreen fund, model portfolio) goes on the approved list; how many private-market providers to carry.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Mutual fund holdings may be switched to cheaper ETF share classes or models.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Offer the client's preferred wrapper where it exists; be honest where a competitor leads (for example active ETFs).
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Approved-list placement is the start: funded assets depend on adviser adoption afterwards.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Support advisers with training and reporting so listed funds are actually used and held appropriately.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Active ETFs are still about 3.4% of European ETF assets; alliance funds are small at launch; fee levels are lower than the vehicles they replace (BCG).
- **A question to ask a client:** Which wrapper do your advisers actually use for new money now, and what would make a fund you already list easier to hold?
- **Indicators to watch:** Morningstar active ETF data; European launches by the alliances; platform shelf changes.
- **Sources:** R8-S14, R8-S16, R8-S24, R8-S40, R8-S39, R8-S38

### Global managers build presence around Gulf sovereign investors `D-R8-09`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Middle East
- **Event date:** 2026-10-19
- **Publication date:** 2026-10-19
- **1 · What happened:** Fourteen firms announced plans for an Abu Dhabi Global Market presence in October 2026; managers there oversee more than $2.1tn globally; Capital Group obtained an ADGM licence. BlackRock is reported to plan up to $100bn of deployment via the region; Mubadala and ADIA are reported to partner with Apollo, Blue Owl and KKR in private credit; PIF anchors new vehicles for managers that open in Riyadh.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Sovereign investors can ask for local presence, co-investment, knowledge transfer and anchor terms; they are partners and competitors for deals as well as clients.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Which managers receive mandates or anchor commitments, and on what conditions (local office, joint ventures).
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** Existing mandates may be consolidated into strategic partnerships.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Coverage needs partnership structures and senior access, not product pushing; local regulation and presence matter.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** Long, relationship-led cycles with bespoke terms; origination credit is shared across the firm.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Servicing includes training, secondments and co-investment flow, beyond reporting.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Regional conflict in 2026 has affected sentiment (BoE refers to the Middle East conflict); headline commitment figures are not funded assets; Goldman-specific Gulf evidence is outside this stream (see R5).
- **A question to ask a client:** Beyond returns, what do you want a manager to build with you locally?
- **Indicators to watch:** ADGM/DIFC/Riyadh licence announcements; sovereign fund annual reports; anchor mandates.
- **Sources:** R8-S57, R8-S58, R8-S52

### Tokenised money market fund shares as collateral `D-R8-10`

- **Evidence status:** Verified
- **Teams:** 
  - Liquidity Solutions
- **Event date:** 2025-07-23
- **Publication date:** 2026-07-13
- **1 · What happened:** BNY and Goldman Sachs launched 'mirror' tokens of MMF shares on Goldman's digital asset platform in July 2025, with funds from BlackRock, Fidelity, Federated Hermes, BNY Dreyfus and GSAM. In July 2026 an industry group (GDF with ISDA) published sandbox work on using tokenised MMFs as collateral.
- **2 · How it affects the client:** Treasurers and pension funds could post MMF shares as margin instead of redeeming to cash, reducing forced selling in stress.
- **3 · What decision changes:** Whether to hold liquidity in vehicles that can be mobilised as collateral; operational readiness and legal certainty.
- **4 · Implications for existing investments:** No change yet for most holders; adoption volumes not established.
- **5 · Possible GSAM response (interpretation, not a GS statement):** (Layer B, not a GS statement) Explain what is live versus pilot; keep the focus on the client's liquidity and collateral problem.
- **6 · Sales responsibility:** A talking point with sophisticated treasury and LDI clients, not yet a mainstream product decision.
- **7 · Relationship-management responsibility:** Help operations teams understand eligibility and custody implications.
- **8 · Risks and counterarguments:** Legal and regulatory acceptance of tokenised collateral is incomplete; competitors participate in the same programme, so it is not exclusive; volumes may stay small.
- **A question to ask a client:** In a margin call, how quickly can you turn your liquidity holdings into eligible collateral today?
- **Indicators to watch:** Regulatory statements on tokenised collateral eligibility; reported volumes; UK and EU MMF reform implementation.
- **Sources:** R8-S55, R8-S56, R8-S52

# Appendix E: Competitors

### BlackRock `K-R8-01`

- **Current business name:** Yes: BlackRock, Inc.
- **Assets:** Above $15.5tn (reported $15.49tn)
- **As at:** 2026-09-30
- **Relevant to which client problem:** 
  - Low-cost index and ETF exposure
  - UK fiduciary management / OCIO
  - LDI
  - Liquidity
  - Insurance general accounts
  - Private markets for institutions and wealth
  - Gulf sovereign partnerships
  - Risk technology (Aladdin)
- **Capabilities:** iShares ETFs ($135bn net inflows in Q3 2026); Aladdin technology; over $400bn/£300bn OCIO and fiduciary assets worldwide (31 Mar 2026); private markets built through GIP and HPS; most frequently named unaffiliated manager by US insurers.
- **Recent events:** Appointed fiduciary manager of the £8bn British Coal Staff Superannuation Scheme (1 Jul 2026). Reported cut of about 1% of staff across investment and sales teams (Jan 2026). Reported plan to steer up to $100bn through a Gulf platform.
- **Where it may be stronger than GSAM:** (Layer B) Index and ETF scale, technology sold to clients, a longer list of very large UK fiduciary mandates, and breadth of insurer relationships.
- **Where GSAM may be stronger (interpretation):** (Layer B) Depth in liquidity and alternatives relative to firm size, an open-architecture manager-selection platform (XIG), and connectivity to a banking and markets franchise. None of these is exclusive to Goldman Sachs.
- **Sources:** R8-S02, R8-S03, R8-S26, R8-S30, R8-S47, R8-S58

# Appendix F: Client lifecycle

### Research and targeting `LC-01`

- **In plain English:** Working out which organisations might have a need the firm can genuinely meet, before anyone picks up the phone.
- **What happens:** The team maps a market: who the asset owners are, how large they are, who advises them, which managers they use, when contracts or strategy reviews fall due, and which searches are open. Public documents do most of the work: annual reports, statements of investment principles, committee papers, consultant search notices and trade press. Prospects are ranked by fit and timing, and some are deliberately set aside.
- **Who leads:** Sales, with consultant relations where a consultant controls access.
- **Who supports:** Analysts, marketing and data teams; relationship managers who know related organisations.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - One-page prospect briefing with sources and a list of what is not known
  - Tracker of contract end dates, strategy reviews and open searches
  - Map of who advises whom (consultant, independent trustee, actuary)
  - CRM clean-up: correct contacts, roles and meeting history
- **On the client side:** Nobody yet. The prospect does not know it is being researched and owes the firm nothing.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client's first conversation is with someone who already understands its structure and constraints and does not waste its time on products that cannot fit.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Targeting by size alone and ignoring fit
  - Out-of-date information presented as current
  - Pursuing a search where the firm's strategy is not rated by the client's consultant
  - Treating a public document as telling the whole story
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Almost entirely an origination activity. Relationship managers contribute intelligence about clients that share a consultant, sponsor or trustee.
- **Timing:** Continuous background work; intensity rises when a search is announced.

### First contact and discovery `LC-02`

- **In plain English:** The first conversations, where the aim is to understand the client's problem, not to present a product.
- **What happens:** An introduction is made directly, through a consultant or at an industry event. Early meetings are mostly questions: what the organisation is trying to achieve, what constrains it (liabilities, liquidity needs, governance time, regulation, beliefs), how decisions are made and by whom, and what has disappointed it before. The firm also decides whether it should compete at all.
- **Who leads:** Sales.
- **Who supports:** A product or investment specialist if the conversation turns technical; senior sponsor for very large prospects.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Question list tailored to the prospect
  - Meeting notes written up the same day, separating what was said from what was inferred
  - Contact map: who decides, who advises, who influences, who operates
  - Follow-up email draft with the material promised
- **On the client side:** Usually an investment officer, pensions manager or chief investment officer; sometimes the consultant alone. They need to know quickly whether this manager is worth further time.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client is asked questions that sharpen its own understanding of the problem, and is told honestly if the firm is not a good fit.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Presenting before listening
  - Speaking only to one contact and mistaking their view for the organisation's
  - Promising follow-up that does not arrive
  - Failing to qualify out: continuing because a meeting was friendly
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Origination. Where the organisation is already a client for another mandate, the relationship manager normally makes the introduction and may lead.
- **Timing:** From a single meeting to many months of intermittent contact.

### Solution design and proposal `LC-03`

- **In plain English:** Turning what was learned into a specific proposal: which strategy, in what form, on what terms.
- **What happens:** Sales works with portfolio managers, product specialists and sometimes a solutions team to shape a proposal: the strategy or combination of strategies, the vehicle (pooled fund or segregated mandate), benchmark and guidelines, reporting, indicative fees. Pricing outside the standard schedule needs internal approval. The proposal should state what the approach will not do as clearly as what it will.
- **Who leads:** Sales, with the investment or solutions team owning the investment content.
- **Who supports:** Product specialists, pricing or business management, legal for unusual terms, compliance for review.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - First draft of proposal slides from approved content
  - Data tables: performance, risk and peer comparisons reconciled to official records
  - Fee illustration on stated assumptions
  - Version control and compliance-review log
- **On the client side:** Investment staff and consultant. They need a proposal that addresses their stated problem and can be compared fairly with others.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client receives a proposal built around its objective and constraints, with trade-offs visible, so it can make an informed comparison.
- **Failure points:** 
  - A generic pitch with the client's name on the cover
  - Fees or terms quoted without approval
  - Performance shown selectively or for a different vehicle than the one proposed
  - Over-engineering: a complex solution to a simple need
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Origination. For an expansion of an existing mandate the relationship manager often leads because they know the guidelines and history.
- **Timing:** Days for a standard product; months for a bespoke or multi-asset solution.

### RFP and due diligence `LC-04`

- **In plain English:** The formal written stage: answering the client's questionnaire and proving that what was said is true.
- **What happens:** The client or its consultant issues a request for proposal (RFP) and a due-diligence questionnaire (DDQ) covering organisation, team, process, performance, risk, operations, compliance, responsible investment and fees. Responses are scored. Operational due diligence may include site visits and interviews with risk, compliance and operations staff. For UK occupational schemes appointing a fiduciary manager above the regulatory threshold, a qualifying competitive tender with at least three unconnected providers is required.
- **Who leads:** Sales owns the submission; the RFP team runs the process.
- **Who supports:** Content owners across the firm (portfolio managers, risk, compliance, operations, legal, human resources), each approving the facts in their area.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Question-by-question tracker with owner, status and deadline
  - First-draft answers from the approved library, updated for the latest quarter
  - Evidence folder: policies, organisation charts, sample reports
  - Consistency check between the RFP, the presentation and the factsheet
- **On the client side:** Consultant's research and operational due-diligence teams, the client's investment staff, sometimes procurement. They need complete, accurate and comparable answers by the deadline.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client can rely on the answers when it justifies its decision, and discovers no surprises after appointment.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Missed deadline or ignored format rules, which can disqualify
  - Stale library answers (old team names, old assets figures)
  - Inconsistency between documents
  - Answering a different question from the one asked
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Origination. In a re-tender the relationship manager is deeply involved because the incumbent's record is part of the evidence.
- **Timing:** Typically weeks, set by the client's timetable.

### Final presentation and appointment `LC-05`

- **In plain English:** The shortlist meeting, often called a beauty parade, after which the decision-makers choose.
- **What happens:** A small number of shortlisted managers present to the decision-making body, such as a trustee board or investment committee, usually for a fixed time with questions. The consultant normally attends and may score. The team presenting is typically the senior salesperson, the portfolio manager and the person who would manage the relationship. The client then decides, sometimes on the day, and informs managers of the outcome.
- **Who leads:** Senior sales with the portfolio manager.
- **Who supports:** The proposed relationship manager, product specialist, team head for large mandates.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Rehearsal pack and timed running order
  - Bank of likely questions with agreed answers and who takes each
  - Biographies and leave-behind document
  - Post-pitch record of every question asked and the outcome, including feedback if lost
- **On the client side:** Trustees or committee members, who may be non-specialists with limited time, plus their consultant. They need to understand the proposal, test the people and feel able to defend the choice.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The decision-makers understand what they are buying and what could go wrong, and meet the people they will actually deal with.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Talking over the audience's level or under it
  - A portfolio manager who cannot explain simply
  - Presenting a team the client will never see again
  - Not asking for feedback after a loss
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Origination, but this is where the relationship manager should first appear so the handover is not a surprise.
- **Timing:** A single meeting; the decision may follow within days or after the next governance meeting.

### Legal negotiation and onboarding `LC-06`

- **In plain English:** Agreeing the contract and opening the accounts so the mandate can legally and operationally begin.
- **What happens:** For a segregated mandate an investment management agreement (IMA) is negotiated: guidelines, benchmark, fees, reporting, liability, termination. For a pooled fund the client completes subscription documents. Client due diligence (know-your-customer and anti-money-laundering checks) is completed. Custody links, trading accounts and reporting are set up and tested.
- **Who leads:** Onboarding or client implementation team with legal; the relationship manager coordinates the client side.
- **Who supports:** Sales (to honour what was promised), portfolio manager (to confirm guidelines are workable), operations, compliance, tax.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Document checklist and status tracker
  - Chasing list for outstanding client and custodian items
  - Comparison of contract terms against the proposal, flagging differences
  - Reporting set-up specification
- **On the client side:** Legal advisers, operations staff, custodian, sometimes the sponsor's treasury. They need a clear list of what is required and prompt answers.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The mandate starts on the agreed date with guidelines that say what the client intended.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Promises made in the pitch that the contract does not reflect
  - Guidelines that are ambiguous or cannot be monitored
  - Slow due-diligence document collection delaying funding
  - No single owner between sales and relationship management
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** The seam. In a handover model responsibility passes here; in a paired or single-owner model the same people continue.
- **Timing:** Weeks to several months, driven by legal negotiation and document collection.

### Funding and transition `LC-07`

- **In plain English:** The money or assets actually arrive and the portfolio is built.
- **What happens:** The client transfers cash or existing securities. If assets come from a previous manager, a transition plan sets out how the old portfolio becomes the new one while controlling cost and market exposure; a specialist transition manager may be used. The portfolio manager invests to the target. Fee accrual starts on funded assets. The first report is produced and checked with unusual care.
- **Who leads:** Operations and the portfolio manager, with the relationship manager as the client's single point of contact.
- **Who supports:** Transition specialists, trading, performance and reporting teams.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Funding timeline with dates and responsible parties
  - Day-one confirmation to the client of what has been received
  - Check of the first report against the contract: benchmark, fee basis, guideline tests
  - Welcome pack: contacts, reporting calendar, escalation route
- **On the client side:** Operations and finance staff, custodian, outgoing manager. They need certainty about dates, amounts and who to call.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client's assets are invested as agreed without avoidable cost, and the first report is right.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Funding later or smaller than announced
  - An error in the first report, which sets the tone
  - Transition costs not explained in advance
  - Performance inception date disputed
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Stewardship begins. An announced mandate is not fee-paying until funded, so sales often stays involved until money arrives.
- **Timing:** Days for cash into a pooled fund; weeks or longer for a complex transition or phased funding.

### Ongoing reviews and reporting `LC-08`

- **In plain English:** The long middle of the relationship: regular reports, review meetings and answering questions.
- **What happens:** Reports go out on a fixed calendar. Review meetings, typically quarterly to annual, cover performance, positioning, risk, guideline compliance, service and outlook. Between meetings there are ad hoc questions, data requests from the consultant, regulatory reporting needs and operational changes. The relationship manager keeps the internal picture of the client current as people and priorities change.
- **Who leads:** Relationship management.
- **Who supports:** Client service and reporting teams, portfolio managers and product specialists for meetings, consultant relations.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Draft review pack reconciled to the official performance record
  - Meeting preparation note
  - Minutes and action log within two working days
  - Query log with response times
- **On the client side:** Investment staff, trustees or committee, consultant, operations and finance. They need accurate information on time and plain explanations.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client understands what it owns and why it has behaved as it has, and can govern the mandate with confidence.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Late or wrong reports
  - Meetings that recite a pack and answer no questions
  - Actions that are not followed up
  - Losing touch when the client's staff change
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Stewardship. Sales stays informed because a well-served client is the main source of references and further mandates.
- **Timing:** Years; this stage is most of the relationship.

### Difficulties `LC-09`

- **In plain English:** Something goes wrong: performance disappoints, an error occurs, or key people leave.
- **What happens:** Common triggers are sustained underperformance, a guideline breach or operational error, the departure of a portfolio manager, a change in the firm's ownership or strategy, or a change on the client's side such as a new consultant. The relationship manager establishes facts, informs the client early, brings the right senior people into the conversation and agrees remedies. Clients may place the manager under formal review or on a watch list.
- **Who leads:** Relationship management, escalating to the team head and senior investment staff.
- **Who supports:** Portfolio manager, risk, compliance and legal for breaches, operations for errors, consultant relations.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Chronology of facts for an incident
  - Draft holding statement and client note for compliance review
  - Attribution summary of what drove underperformance
  - Tracker of clients affected and who has been contacted
- **On the client side:** Chief investment officer or pensions manager, trustee chair, consultant. They need the truth quickly, a clear account of cause and remedy, and evidence that it will not recur.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client hears bad news first from the manager, understands it, and can make a reasoned decision to stay, reduce or leave.
- **Failure points:** 
  - The client learning of a problem from the press or its consultant
  - Defensive or evasive explanations
  - Predicting a recovery that cannot be promised
  - Compensation or remediation handled slowly
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** Stewardship under stress. A badly handled difficulty also damages origination, because consultants remember.
- **Timing:** Unpredictable; a formal review can last several quarters.

### Additional allocations and cross-sell `LC-10`

- **In plain English:** The client considers giving the manager more money or a second, different mandate.
- **What happens:** A need emerges from the client's changing strategy, for instance moving towards lower-risk assets, adding private markets or consolidating managers. The relationship manager recognises it, tests whether the firm has a suitable capability, and introduces the right colleagues. Even for an existing client a larger or new mandate may need a formal process. Sometimes the right conclusion is that the client should not add.
- **Who leads:** Relationship management or sales, according to the firm's coverage model.
- **Who supports:** Product specialists for the new capability, the original salesperson, pricing for relationship-level fee terms.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Opportunity note: the client's stated need, evidence and timing
  - Updated client map and exposure summary across all mandates
  - Fee illustration including any breakpoint effect of larger assets
  - Briefing for the specialist who has never met the client
- **On the client side:** Investment staff and consultant. They need a proposal that reflects what the manager already knows about them and respects any limit on exposure to one firm.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client gets a relevant idea at the right time from people who understand its situation, and is not pestered with irrelevant ones.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Pitching while an existing problem is unresolved
  - Proposing a product because it is a firm priority, not because it fits
  - Internal dispute over credit delaying the response
  - Ignoring the client's manager-concentration limits
- **Sales versus relationship management (illustrative; varies):** The clearest overlap between origination and stewardship. Firms differ on who leads and who is credited.
- **Timing:** Opportunistic; depends on the client's strategy cycle.

### Formal review or re-tender `LC-11`

- **In plain English:** The client deliberately tests whether the manager should keep the mandate.
- **What happens:** Many asset owners review managers on a fixed cycle or after a trigger. The review may be a desk-based assessment by the consultant, a re-presentation to trustees, or a full re-tender against competitors. Public-sector bodies may be required to re-procure. The incumbent must show its record, how it handled problems, and why it remains the right choice for the client's current needs, which may have changed since appointment.
- **Who leads:** Relationship management, with sales where the review is a competitive tender.
- **Who supports:** Portfolio manager, RFP team, consultant relations, team head.
- **What the junior person produces:** 
  - Incumbent record: performance, service statistics, incidents and how they were resolved
  - Summary of how the client's needs have changed since appointment
  - Re-tender response using the client's own history, not library text
  - Fee comparison and options for approval
- **On the client side:** Trustees or committee, consultant, sometimes procurement. They need a fair basis for comparison and may want to see the manager challenged.
- **What good execution changes for the client:** The client reaffirms or changes its decision on current evidence, with the incumbent having given an honest account of its record.
- **Failure points:** 
  - Complacency: treating the review as a formality
  - Defending the original mandate when the client's needs have moved on
