Many are answered from a maintained library and routed to subject experts. *Why it matters:* Completing and tracking DDQs accurately is a routine, high-volume part of client service.
- **Dealing cut-off**: The time of day by which an order to buy or sell fund units must arrive to be processed that day. *Mechanics:* For MMFs, late cut-offs and same-day settlement are selling points. *Why it matters:* A practical detail treasurers care about more than yield.
- **Default fund**: The investment a pension saver is put into automatically if they make no choice. *Mechanics:* Holds the large majority of DC assets. Subject to a charge cap in the UK for auto-enrolment schemes. *Why it matters:* Winning a place inside a default strategy is the main prize in DC.
- **Derivative**: A contract whose value depends on something else, such as an interest rate or share index. *Mechanics:* Futures, options, swaps, forwards. Used for hedging and efficient exposure. Use is limited by guidelines. *Why it matters:* Clients ask what derivatives are for and what could go wrong.
- **DFM** (Discretionary fund manager): A firm that makes investment decisions for clients without asking permission for each trade. *Mechanics:* In UK wealth, DFMs run bespoke and model portfolios for advisers' clients. They are professional fund buyers. *Why it matters:* A major buyer group in UK third-party wealth.
- **Discount rate**: The interest rate used to turn future payments into a value today. *Mechanics:* A lower rate makes distant liabilities worth more today. Usually tied to gilt or high-quality bond yields. *Why it matters:* The reason interest rates move pension liabilities.
- **Distribution** (Also: client business, client coverage): The part of an asset manager that finds, wins, serves and keeps clients. *Mechanics:* Includes sales, relationship management, consultant relations, product specialists, RFP and client service teams. Names vary by firm. *Why it matters:* It is the function this project studies.
- **Distribution (private funds)**: Money a private fund pays back to investors when it sells an investment or receives income. *Mechanics:* Not to be confused with 'distribution' meaning sales. Timing is uncertain and depends on exit markets. *Why it matters:* Slow distributions reduce clients' ability to make new commitments.
- **Drawdown**: The fall in value from a high point to the next low point. *Mechanics:* Maximum drawdown is the worst such fall over a period. Also used, confusingly, for capital calls and for taking pension income. *Why it matters:* It is how clients experience loss.
- **Duration**: A measure of how much a bond's price changes when interest rates change. *Mechanics:* Roughly, a bond with a duration of 15 years falls about 15% if yields rise by one percentage point. Liabilities have duration too. *Why it matters:* The key number for matching assets to pension liabilities.
- **E&F** (Endowments and foundations): Charitable pots of money invested to fund a mission over the long term. *Mechanics:* Aim to preserve real value while spending a steady percentage each year. Governed by trustees or an investment committee. *Why it matters:* A distinct institutional segment with long horizons and mission-related constraints.
- **Effective fee rate**: The average fee a client actually pays across all its assets, after tiers and discounts. *Mechanics:* Total annual fee divided by average assets. With tiered fees it falls as the mandate grows. At firm level it falls when the mix shifts towards cheaper products such as passive or liquidity. *Why it matters:* Lets you compare mandates and see why asset growth does not always mean equal revenue growth.
- **ELTIF** (European long-term investment fund): An EU fund type that lets a wider range of investors access long-term private assets. *Mechanics:* Revised rules, often called ELTIF 2.0, loosened earlier restrictions. Used for semi-liquid private-market funds for wealth clients. *Why it matters:* The EU counterpart to the LTAF in private-markets-for-wealth conversations.
- **Endgame**: A DB scheme's intended final destination: insure the liabilities, or keep running the scheme. *Mechanics:* Options include buy-in, buyout, run-on and consolidation vehicles. The choice drives asset allocation and liquidity needs. *Why it matters:* Determines what a scheme will and will not buy from an asset manager.
- **Equities**: Shares: part-ownership of companies. *Mechanics:* Higher long-term expected return with larger falls along the way. *Why it matters:* The growth engine in DC and endowment portfolios.
- **ESG** (Environmental, social and governance): Considering how companies treat the environment, people and their own management when investing. *Mechanics:* Ranges from exclusions to integration to impact strategies. Client requirements and regulation differ sharply by region. *Why it matters:* A standard section of RFPs and DDQs; clients' positions vary and must not be assumed.
- **ETF** (Exchange-traded fund): A fund you buy and sell on a stock exchange like a share. *Mechanics:* Most track an index cheaply; active ETFs are growing. Trades throughout the day at market price, close to NAV. *Why it matters:* A major channel for low-cost investing and a source of fee pressure on traditional funds.
- **Family office**: A private firm that manages the wealth of one very rich family, or a few. *Mechanics:* Sits between institutional and wealth clients: professional staff, flexible mandate, interest in private deals. *Why it matters:* Coverage may sit in either an institutional or a wealth team.
- **FCA** (Financial Conduct Authority): The UK regulator of how financial firms behave towards customers and markets. *Mechanics:* Authorises asset managers and funds; sets conduct rules including the Consumer Duty. *Why it matters:* Its rules shape what can be said and sold to whom.
- **Fiduciary duty**: A legal duty to act in someone else's best interests, not your own. *Mechanics:* Trustees owe it to beneficiaries. Managers owe contractual and regulatory duties to clients. It is why trust never replaces due diligence. *Why it matters:* Frames what a client may properly decide and why process matters.
- **Fixed income**: Investments that pay set interest and return the money lent: bonds and loans. *Mechanics:* Government bonds, corporate credit, securitised debt, emerging market debt. The largest asset class for pensions and insurers. *Why it matters:* Most institutional mandates in the UK are fixed income or liability-related.
- **FM** (Fiduciary management): The usual UK pensions name for OCIO. *Mechanics:* Trustees must run a qualifying competitive tender, with bids from at least three unconnected providers, before fiduciary management first covers 20% or more of scheme assets. *Why it matters:* The regulated tender process shapes how UK OCIO business is won.
- **Fund of funds**: A fund that invests in other funds instead of directly in shares or bonds. *Mechanics:* Its manager is a professional fund buyer. Adds a layer of fees in exchange for selection and diversification. *Why it matters:* Another type of intermediary buyer.
- **Funding level**: A scheme's assets as a percentage of the value of what it has promised to pay. *Mechanics:* Depends heavily on how liabilities are valued. The same scheme has different funding levels on technical provisions, PPF and buyout measures. *Why it matters:* The scorecard trustees care about most.
- **Gatekeeper** (Also: fund selector): The person or team at an intermediary that decides which funds its advisers are allowed to use. *Mechanics:* Runs research and due diligence and maintains the approved list. Approval permits purchase; it does not cause it. *Why it matters:* The first, but not the last, person a wholesale salesperson must convince.
- **General account**: The pool of an insurer's own investments that backs its promises to policyholders. *Mechanics:* Distinct from unit-linked funds, where the policyholder bears the investment risk. *Why it matters:* The portfolio an insurance asset management team is hired to manage.
- **Gilt**: A bond issued by the UK government. *Mechanics:* Conventional gilts pay fixed interest. Long-dated gilts are the main matching asset for UK pension liabilities. *Why it matters:* Gilt yields set the value of UK pension liabilities.
- **GMRA** (Global Master Repurchase Agreement): The standard legal contract under which repo trades are done. *Mechanics:* Sets collateral eligibility, margining and what happens on default. Negotiated per counterparty. *Why it matters:* Clients ask about eligible collateral and margin terms under it.
- **Gross flows**: All the new money won in a period, before subtracting what left. *Mechanics:* Gross sales can be high while net flows are negative if existing clients are withdrawing more than new clients add. *Why it matters:* Explains why winning new business and keeping existing business are both needed.
- **Hedge**: An investment made to reduce a risk you already have. *Mechanics:* A good hedge loses money when the thing it protects gains. That is success, not failure. *Why it matters:* Explaining hedge losses in plain terms is a recurring client conversation.
- **High yield**: Bonds from riskier borrowers that pay more interest. *Mechanics:* Rated below investment grade. Higher default risk and more equity-like behaviour. *Why it matters:* Often restricted or capped in institutional guidelines.
- **High-water mark**: A rule that the manager earns a performance fee only on gains above the fund's previous highest value. *Mechanics:* Prevents a client paying twice for the same gain after a fall and recovery. *Why it matters:* A standard question in fee discussions for strategies with performance fees.
- **HireVue**: A platform for recorded video interviews where candidates answer set questions on camera. *Mechanics:* Used by many large employers as an early screening stage. How any firm reviews recordings should be taken from its own official guidance. *Why it matters:* Likely to be the first interview stage a candidate meets.
- **Hurdle rate**: The minimum return investors must receive before the manager can take a share of profits. *Mechanics:* In private funds often called the preferred return. Carried interest is paid only once it is cleared. *Why it matters:* Determines when, and whether, performance-related fees are earned.
- **IC** (Investment committee): The group that makes or approves investment decisions for an organisation. *Mechanics:* Meets periodically; works from papers prepared by staff or advisers. Its timetable sets the pace of any sale. *Why it matters:* Knowing the committee calendar is basic account planning.
- **IFA** (Independent financial adviser): An adviser who recommends financial products from across the whole market. *Mechanics:* Many now outsource investment selection to DFMs or model portfolios. *Why it matters:* The end of the UK advised distribution chain.
- **IMA** (Investment management agreement): The contract between a client and an asset manager for a segregated mandate. *Mechanics:* Covers objective, guidelines, fees, reporting, liability and termination. Often terminable on short notice. *Why it matters:* Negotiating and then living within it is where the relationship becomes concrete.
- **Incentive fees**: Extra fees GS earns when a fund or account beats an agreed return or benchmark. *Mechanics:* A percentage of return or of excess return, recognised only when significant reversal is not probable. Includes 'overrides' on private equity and credit funds. $489m in 2025; $882m in the first nine months of 2026. *Why it matters:* Lumpy and tied to realisations, so it should not be treated like recurring management fees in a worked example.
- **Index**: A published list of securities and weights that represents a market. *Mechanics:* Built by an index provider to stated rules. The basis for passive funds and most benchmarks. *Why it matters:* You cannot discuss performance without naming the index.
- **Index-linked gilt**: A UK government bond whose payments rise with inflation. *Mechanics:* Used to match pension promises that increase with inflation. *Why it matters:* A core LDI building block.
- **Information asymmetry**: When one side of a deal knows more than the other. *Mechanics:* The manager knows more about its process; the client knows more about its own needs and constraints. *Why it matters:* Much of client coverage is reducing it honestly in both directions.
- **Information ratio**: Outperformance divided by tracking error: how much extra return per unit of active risk. *Mechanics:* A ratio of 0.5 sustained over time is generally considered strong (illustrative rule of thumb). *Why it matters:* A compact way consultants compare active managers.
- **Infrastructure**: Investments in essential physical assets such as energy networks, transport and data centres. *Mechanics:* Long-lived, often inflation-linked cash flows. *Why it matters:* Fits long-horizon investors and features in UK policy debates on pension investment.
- **Institutional investor**: An organisation that invests large sums on behalf of others, such as a pension scheme or insurer. *Mechanics:* Decisions are made by committees under formal governance, often with a consultant. Mandates are large and individually negotiated. *Why it matters:* One of the two main client groups in client coverage, with its own sales cycle and service model.
- **Intermediary**: A professional firm that chooses investments for its own clients, such as a private bank or wealth manager. *Mechanics:* The asset manager's customer is the intermediary; the money belongs to the intermediary's clients. Reached through wholesale or third-party distribution. *Why it matters:* Selling to an intermediary means winning approval and then usage.
- **Investment consultant**: A firm that advises asset owners on investment strategy and on which managers to hire. *Mechanics:* Researches and rates strategies, runs manager searches and monitors appointed managers. Paid by the client. *Why it matters:* An adviser to your client and a judge of your product at the same time.
- **Investment grade**: Bonds from borrowers judged likely to repay. *Mechanics:* Rated BBB-/Baa3 or above by the main rating agencies. Many guidelines require it. *Why it matters:* A common guideline boundary.
- **Investment guidelines**: The client's written rules for what the manager is aiming for and what it must not do. *Mechanics:* Benchmark, permitted assets, limits, exclusions. Monitored by compliance systems. Breaches, even passive ones, must be reported and fixed. *Why it matters:* Coverage staff relay the client's intent accurately when guidelines are drafted and communicate promptly when one is breached.
- **Investment horizon**: How long money can stay invested before it is needed. *Mechanics:* Set by the liabilities. The governance horizon is often shorter than the stated one. *Why it matters:* Keeps performance conversations in the right time frame.
- **ISDA** (International Swaps and Derivatives Association (master agreement)): The standard legal contract for trading derivatives between two parties. *Mechanics:* Supplemented by a credit support annex that sets collateral terms. *Why it matters:* Needed before a mandate can use swaps; often on the onboarding critical path.
- **Journey plan** (Also: flight path): A scheme's plan for getting from its current funding level to its long-term target. *Mechanics:* Sets how risk is reduced as funding improves, often with pre-agreed triggers. *Why it matters:* The reference point for most strategic conversations with a DB scheme.
- **Key-person risk**: The danger that something valuable depends on one individual. *Mechanics:* Applies to portfolio managers (the strategy) and to coverage staff or client contacts (the relationship). Consultants often treat a departure as a trigger for review. *Why it matters:* Explains why firms want multiple contacts and written records for every client.
- **KYC** (Know your customer): Checks a firm must make to confirm who a client really is before doing business with it. *Mechanics:* Identity, ownership and control, source of funds. Part of anti-money-laundering rules. *Why it matters:* A frequent cause of onboarding delay; coverage chases the documents.
- **LDI** (Liability-driven investment): Investing so that your assets move in step with what you owe. *Mechanics:* Uses gilts, index-linked gilts, repo and swaps to hedge interest rate and inflation risk. Often leveraged, which creates collateral calls when yields rise. *Why it matters:* Central to UK DB relationships; the 2022 gilt episode still shapes liquidity discussions.
- **Leverage**: Using borrowed money or derivatives to get more exposure than the cash you put in. *Mechanics:* Magnifies gains and losses and creates a need for collateral. *Why it matters:* The source of the liquidity stress in LDI in 2022.
- **LGPS** (Local Government Pension Scheme): The pension scheme for local government workers; unlike most public-sector schemes it holds real investments. *Mechanics:* In England and Wales it is run through 86 administering authorities that invest via shared pools, which are being consolidated. *Why it matters:* A large UK institutional segment where the buyer is increasingly the pool.
- **LGPS pool**: A shared investment company owned by a group of LGPS funds to invest their money together. *Mechanics:* Pools select and oversee external managers or manage in-house. Government reforms are reducing the number of pools and making pooling compulsory. *Why it matters:* Changes who an external manager must win over, and concentrates decisions.
- **Liability**: Something you owe, now or in the future. *Mechanics:* For a pension scheme, the promised pensions; for an insurer, expected claims. *Why it matters:* Client needs are driven by liabilities more than by a wish for return.
- **Liquidity**: How quickly an investment can be turned into cash without a poor price. *Mechanics:* Also used for the cash-management business line. Liquidity can vanish in stressed markets. *Why it matters:* Shapes what a client can hold and what it needs in a crisis.
- **Liquidity mismatch**: When a fund lets investors leave faster than it can sell its investments. *Mechanics:* Can force dealing suspensions. A focus of regulators for property and credit funds. *Why it matters:* A risk clients and gatekeepers test for.
- **Liquidity products**: Cash-like holdings: money market funds and private bank deposits. *Mechanics:* A GS AUS asset class. $1,051bn at 30 Sep 2026; average effective fee 13 bps for nine months 2026. *Why it matters:* Large balances and low fees, and flows can reverse quickly. The reported class mixes money market funds with private bank deposits, so it is not a clean measure of the Liquidity Solutions fund business.
- **Long-term AUS** (Long-term assets under supervision): AUS excluding cash-like liquidity products: alternatives, equity and fixed income. *Mechanics:* GS reports net flows and the client-channel split (Institutional, Wealth management, Third-party distributed) on long-term AUS only. $3,046bn at 30 Sep 2026. *Why it matters:* When GS says 'long-term fee-based net inflows' it means flows into these assets, which carry higher fees than liquidity products.
- **LTAF** (Long-term asset fund): A UK fund type designed to hold illiquid investments such as private equity and infrastructure. *Mechanics:* An open-ended authorised fund with long notice periods for withdrawals. Aimed at DC pensions and, with conditions, wealth investors. *Why it matters:* One route by which private markets reach UK pension savers.
- **LVNAV** (Low volatility net asset value): A money market fund that deals at a steady price so long as the true value of its holdings stays very close to it. *Mechanics:* If the stable price deviates from market value by more than 20 basis points, dealing switches to the market-based price. *Why it matters:* The most widely used prime MMF structure in Europe for corporate cash.
- **Management and other fees**: The AWM revenue line for fees charged on client assets and related services. *Mechanics:* Mostly asset-based fees on managed client assets, plus advisory, planning and brokerage fees for wealth clients. $11,538m in 2025, of which Asset management $4,990m and Wealth management $6,548m. *Why it matters:* The recurring fee line that asset gathering and retention feed. Recurring does not mean guaranteed: it moves with markets, flows and mix.
- **Management fee**: The regular fee an asset manager charges, set as a yearly percentage of the money it manages. *Mechanics:* Accrues on average assets and is billed monthly or quarterly. It is gross revenue, not profit. Private market funds often charge it on committed or invested capital instead of NAV. *Why it matters:* It is the recurring income that client coverage helps to win and keep.
- **Mandate**: An instruction from a client to an asset manager to run a specific pot of money in a specific way. *Mechanics:* Used loosely for any appointment. Check whether it is awarded, funded, and fee-paying: these are different stages. *Why it matters:* Press reports of 'mandate wins' often describe awards, not money that has arrived.
- **Master trust**: A single DC pension scheme used by many unrelated employers. *Mechanics:* Run by a professional trustee board and authorised by TPR. Large master trusts are among the most significant DC buyers. *Why it matters:* A concentrated, professional buyer for DC investment capabilities.
- **Matching adjustment**: A rule that lets insurers value long-term promises more favourably if they hold assets with matching, predictable cash flows. *Mechanics:* Central to UK annuity business. Eligibility rules determine which assets insurers want. *Why it matters:* Drives UK insurers' demand for long-dated and private credit.
- **MFN clause** (Most-favoured-nation clause): A promise that a client's fee will be at least as good as that of any similar client. *Mechanics:* Usually limited to comparable strategy, size and service. One discount elsewhere can trigger repricing for MFN holders. *Why it matters:* Explains why a fee concession to one client can cost far more than it appears.
- **MiFID II** (Markets in Financial Instruments Directive II): European rules on how investment firms deal with clients and markets. *Mechanics:* Covers client classification, suitability, cost disclosure, inducements and research payments. The UK has its own version. *Why it matters:* Underlies client categories such as professional and retail.
- **MMF** (Money market fund): A fund that holds very short-term, high-quality debt to keep cash safe and available. *Mechanics:* An investment, not a deposit, with no deposit guarantee. Low fees, very large balances. UK and EU rules recognise three structures. *Why it matters:* The core product of a liquidity solutions business.
- **MPS** (Model portfolio service): A ready-made portfolio of funds that advisers can apply to many clients at once. *Mechanics:* Run by a DFM or asset manager. One decision by the model's manager moves money for thousands of clients. *Why it matters:* Being selected into a model is often worth more than being on a buy list.
- **Multi-asset**: A portfolio that mixes shares, bonds and other investments under one manager. *Mechanics:* The manager also decides the mix. Used in DC defaults, wealth portfolios and OCIO. *Why it matters:* A solutions product that depends on asset allocation skill.
- **NAV** (Net asset value): What a fund is worth: its assets minus what it owes. Divided by the number of units, it gives the price of one unit. *Mechanics:* Calculated daily for most public funds and quarterly, by estimate, for most private funds. Dealing in a pooled fund happens at NAV per unit. *Why it matters:* Fund fees, performance and client statements are all based on it.
- **NAV-based fee**: A fee calculated as a percentage of the current value of the client's investment. *Mechanics:* Standard for public-market funds and mandates. The fee rises and falls with markets as well as with flows. *Why it matters:* Explains why a market fall cuts revenue even when no client leaves.
- **Net flows**: New client money coming in minus client money going out over a period. *Mechanics:* Gross inflows minus redemptions and withdrawals. Reported separately from market movements, so it shows whether clients are adding or leaving regardless of what markets did. *Why it matters:* It is the cleanest public measure of whether a client business is winning and keeping assets.
- **Non-fee-earning alternative assets**: Alternative assets GS controls or has commitments for but does not currently charge management fees on. *Mechanics:* Mainly balance-sheet investments, unfunded commitments (GS's own and clients' where fees are not charged on commitments), fund credit facilities and employee funds. $207bn at 31 Dec 2025 against alternative AUS of $420bn. *Why it matters:* Explains why a commitment a client signs today may not produce fee revenue until capital is drawn and invested.
- **OCF** (Ongoing charges figure): The yearly cost of owning a fund, shown as a percentage. *Mechanics:* Includes the management fee plus administration, custody and similar running costs. Excludes dealing costs inside the fund and any performance fee. *Why it matters:* The number fund selectors and advisers compare first.
- **OCIO** (Outsourced chief investment officer): An arrangement where an organisation hands day-to-day investment decisions for all or most of its portfolio to a specialist provider. *Mechanics:* The client keeps objectives and risk appetite; the provider handles asset allocation within limits, manager selection, implementation and reporting. Measured against the client's own objective. *Why it matters:* A different business model from single-strategy mandates: a governance sale, a whole-portfolio relationship.
- **ODD** (Operational due diligence): A client's check that a manager's operations, controls and systems are sound, separate from whether its investing is good. *Mechanics:* Covers valuation, trading controls, business continuity, cyber security, service providers. A failed ODD can veto an otherwise attractive strategy. *Why it matters:* Explains why winning a mandate involves far more people than the portfolio manager.
- **OEIC** (Open-ended investment company): The usual legal form for UK-based funds. *Mechanics:* The UK counterpart to a SICAV. Creates and cancels shares as investors come and go. *Why it matters:* Appears in UK fund names and platform listings.
- **Official institution**: A central bank, government agency or similar public body that invests reserves. *Mechanics:* Very conservative, focused on safety and liquidity, with strict eligibility rules. *Why it matters:* A specialist client segment often covered alongside sovereign funds.
- **Onboarding**: Everything that has to happen between a client saying yes and its money being invested. *Mechanics:* Legal agreement, anti-money-laundering checks, custody and market account openings, guideline coding, reporting set-up, transition of assets. *Why it matters:* Delays here postpone revenue and shape the client's first impression.
- **Overrides**: GS's word for its larger share of profits on a private fund once returns pass a threshold; commonly called carried interest. *Mechanics:* Defined in the 10-K as 'the increased share of the income and gains derived primarily from our private equity and credit funds when the return on a fund's investments over the life of the fund exceeds certain threshold returns'. *Why it matters:* Use GS's own term when discussing its disclosures; a client-side reader will usually say carried interest.
- **Passive management** (Also: index tracking): Trying to match a benchmark as closely and cheaply as possible. *Mechanics:* Very low fees, huge scale. Has taken market share from active equity for many years. *Why it matters:* The main source of fee pressure and the default alternative a client compares you with.
- **Performance fee**: An extra fee paid only if the manager beats an agreed target. *Mechanics:* Usually a share of returns above a benchmark or hurdle, often subject to a high-water mark. Makes revenue more variable. *Why it matters:* Clients ask how it is calculated and whether it rewards skill or just rising markets.
- **Pipeline**: The list of potential deals a sales team is working on, with their stage and likelihood. *Mechanics:* Stages typically run from lead through proposal and finals to won, funded or lost. *Why it matters:* Pipeline reviews are a regular work product.
- **Platform**: An online service where advisers and investors hold and trade funds. *Mechanics:* Provides custody, dealing and reporting. A fund must be available on the platforms an intermediary uses before it can be bought. *Why it matters:* A practical precondition for wholesale sales.
- **PLSA** (Pensions and Lifetime Savings Association): The main trade body for UK workplace pension schemes. *Mechanics:* Runs major industry conferences and lobbies on pensions policy. The body has announced a change of name to Pensions UK; the date was not verified in this stream, so check current usage. *Why it matters:* Its events are where UK institutional coverage teams meet clients and consultants.
- **PM** (Portfolio manager): The person who decides what a portfolio buys and sells. *Mechanics:* Accountable for performance within the guidelines. Their time with clients is rationed. *Why it matters:* Coverage decides when a client meeting justifies the PM's time.
- **Pooled fund**: A shared pot: many investors put money into one fund that is managed as a single portfolio. *Mechanics:* Investors own units, deal at NAV and accept the fund's rules. Cheaper and quicker to enter than a segregated mandate, with no customisation. *Why it matters:* The standard vehicle in wealth distribution and for smaller institutions.
- **PPF** (Pension Protection Fund): The UK safety net that pays compensation to members of DB schemes whose employer has failed. *Mechanics:* Funded by a levy and its own investments. Publishes the Purple Book, the standard data source on UK DB schemes. *Why it matters:* Its data is the best public picture of the UK DB market.
- **PRA** (Prudential Regulation Authority): The part of the Bank of England that supervises the financial soundness of banks and insurers. *Mechanics:* Sets Solvency UK requirements for insurers. *Why it matters:* Its rules shape insurance clients' investment choices.
- **Principal–agent problem**: The difficulty that arises when one party relies on another to act for it but cannot fully check what the other does. *Mechanics:* Addressed through contracts, monitoring, incentives and reputation. Runs through the whole chain from member to manager. *Why it matters:* Explains why reporting, due diligence and guidelines exist.
- **Private bank**: A bank division that manages money for wealthy individuals and families. *Mechanics:* Selects third-party funds centrally and distributes them through its bankers. Often global buying decisions with local implementation. *Why it matters:* A core third-party wealth client type.
- **Private credit**: Lending directly to companies or projects instead of buying bonds in the market. *Mechanics:* Offers extra yield for illiquidity and complexity. Popular with insurers and pension schemes seeking income. *Why it matters:* A fast-growing area in institutional and insurance conversations.
- **Private equity**: Buying companies that are not listed on a stock exchange, improving them and selling them. *Mechanics:* Funds last around ten years; returns come mostly at exit. *Why it matters:* A flagship alternatives product.
- **Private markets**: Investments negotiated directly and not traded on an exchange. *Mechanics:* Accessed through closed-end or semi-liquid funds with commitments, capital calls and long holding periods. *Why it matters:* Changes the fee base, the liquidity conversation and the client's due diligence.
- **Product specialist** (Also: investment specialist, client portfolio manager (CPM)): An investment expert who explains a strategy to clients but does not usually make the investment decisions. *Mechanics:* Sits between portfolio managers and coverage. Presents at pitches and reviews so portfolio managers can stay focused on investing. *Why it matters:* The colleague coverage relies on for technical depth in meetings.
- **Public markets**: Markets where securities trade openly with visible prices. *Mechanics:* Listed shares and most bonds. Daily valuation and liquidity. *Why it matters:* The contrast case for private markets.
- **Re-tender test**: Ask whether the client would choose the manager again in a fresh, fully informed competition. *Mechanics:* A reasoning device used in this dossier (not an industry standard term) to separate retention that reflects value from retention that reflects inertia. *Why it matters:* Gives a concrete way to challenge the claim that retention proves a good relationship.
- **Rebate**: Part of a fund's fee paid back to a large investor or intermediary. *Mechanics:* A way to deliver a negotiated price inside a pooled fund. Restricted in some markets and channels by rules on inducements. *Why it matters:* Comes up in wholesale and platform pricing discussions.
- **Repo** (Repurchase agreement): Selling a bond today with a promise to buy it back later: in effect, a short-term loan secured on the bond. *Mechanics:* The main way LDI portfolios obtain leverage on gilts. *Why it matters:* Explains how pension schemes borrow to hedge.
- **Retail investor**: An individual investing their own money. *Mechanics:* Has the strongest regulatory protection. Usually reached through advisers and platforms, not directly. *Why it matters:* The end customer behind most wealth-channel sales.
- **RFI** (Request for information): A shorter, earlier questionnaire used to build a long list of possible managers. *Mechanics:* Often precedes an RFP. Less binding, but still a written record. *Why it matters:* An early signal that a search is under way.
- **RFP** (Request for proposal): A formal invitation to bid for a mandate, with a long list of questions the manager must answer in writing. *Mechanics:* Covers firm, team, process, performance, risk, operations, fees. Answers must be accurate and consistent with every other document the firm has issued. *Why it matters:* Drafting and coordinating RFP responses is a core junior task in a client business.
- **RM** (Relationship management / relationship manager): The role focused on looking after existing clients. *Mechanics:* Reviews, reporting, issue resolution, account planning, and identifying further needs. Often shares responsibility for growth. *Why it matters:* The other track; in practice overlaps with sales.
- **Run-on**: Choosing to keep a well-funded DB scheme going instead of passing it to an insurer. *Mechanics:* The scheme invests to pay pensions over time and may generate surplus. Needs ongoing governance and investment management. *Why it matters:* Keeps assets with managers for longer and changes what trustees want.
- **Sales** (Also: business development): The role focused on winning new clients and new money. *Mechanics:* Prospecting, discovery, proposals, RFP leadership, negotiation through to funding. Boundaries with relationship management vary by firm. *Why it matters:* One of the two tracks a candidate may be placed in.
- **SCR** (Solvency capital requirement): The amount of capital an insurer must hold to survive a severe year. *Mechanics:* Calculated under Solvency II by standard formula or internal model. Riskier assets need more capital. *Why it matters:* Explains insurers' preference for high-quality, capital-efficient assets.
- **Search costs**: The time and effort needed to find and assess a suitable provider. *Mechanics:* Thousands of strategies exist; consultants, ratings and coverage teams all reduce the cost of finding the right one. *Why it matters:* One reason distribution exists.
- **Segregated mandate** (Also: separate account, SMA): A private portfolio run for one client under rules written for that client. *Mechanics:* Assets sit in the client's own custody account. Governed by an IMA and guidelines. Needs scale because of legal and operational set-up cost. *Why it matters:* The typical vehicle for larger institutional clients and the reason onboarding is a project.
- **SFDR** (Sustainable Finance Disclosure Regulation): EU rules on how funds must describe their sustainability characteristics. *Mechanics:* Funds are commonly referred to by the article they disclose under. The regime has been under review. *Why it matters:* European intermediaries filter funds by it.
- **Share class**: A version of a fund with its own fee level, currency or minimum investment. *Mechanics:* All classes own the same portfolio. Institutional and 'clean' classes carry lower charges than legacy retail classes. *Why it matters:* Much wholesale pricing negotiation is about which share class an intermediary can access.
- **SICAV** (Société d'investissement à capital variable): A common legal form for European funds, often based in Luxembourg. *Mechanics:* An open-ended investment company whose share capital varies with investor flows. Many cross-border UCITS are Luxembourg SICAVs. *Why it matters:* You will see it in fund names and documents.
- **Side letter**: A separate agreement giving one investor in a fund special terms. *Mechanics:* Typical contents: fee terms, extra reporting, MFN rights. Common in private funds. *Why it matters:* Shows how individual negotiation happens even inside pooled vehicles.
- **SLA** (Service level agreement): An agreed standard for service, such as how quickly reports are delivered. *Mechanics:* May sit in the IMA or alongside it. Missed SLAs are tracked and reported. *Why it matters:* Turns 'reliability' into something measurable.
- **Solvency II / Solvency UK**: The rules that decide how much capital European and UK insurers must hold against their risks. *Mechanics:* Each asset carries a capital charge depending on its risk. The UK's reformed version is called Solvency UK. *Why it matters:* Insurers judge investments on return after capital cost, not headline yield.
- **Sponsor** (Sponsoring employer): The company that stands behind a DB pension scheme and must fund any shortfall. *Mechanics:* Its interests can differ from the trustees': it bears the cost and may share in any surplus. *Why it matters:* A second party whose view shapes scheme decisions.
- **Stewardship**: How an investor uses its ownership rights: voting and engaging with companies. *Mechanics:* Clients ask for voting records and engagement examples. *Why it matters:* A regular reporting and due diligence topic.
- **Sub-advisory**: One firm hires another to manage the investments of a fund sold under the first firm's name. *Mechanics:* The sub-adviser earns a negotiated fee; the brand owner keeps the client relationship. *Why it matters:* A way to win assets from wealth firms without selling a branded fund.
- **Sub-custodian**: A local bank that holds assets in a particular country on behalf of the main custodian. *Mechanics:* Each market a segregated account will trade in needs an account opened through the local sub-custodian, with local documentation. *Why it matters:* Market account openings are a common critical path item before a mandate can trade.
- **Suitability**: Whether an investment fits the client's objectives, constraints and understanding. *Mechanics:* A regulatory requirement when advising retail clients and a professional standard everywhere. *Why it matters:* The test for when not to sell.
- **Swap**: A contract to exchange one stream of payments for another, such as fixed interest for floating. *Mechanics:* Interest rate and inflation swaps are used to hedge liabilities without buying bonds outright. *Why it matters:* A core LDI instrument.
- **SWF** (Sovereign wealth fund): A state-owned investment fund, often built from oil, gas or trade surpluses. *Mechanics:* Very large, long-horizon, staffed by professionals. Often invests directly and through co-investments as well as through managers. *Why it matters:* A central client type in Middle East coverage.
- **Switching costs**: The cost and disruption of moving from one provider to another. *Mechanics:* Transition costs, governance time, legal work, operational risk. Can keep a client in place for reasons unrelated to quality. *Why it matters:* Retention caused by inertia is not the same as a satisfied client.
- **TCFD** (Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures): A framework for reporting climate-related risks. *Mechanics:* Large UK pension schemes must report in line with it, so they request data from managers. *Why it matters:* Generates recurring client data requests.
- **TER** (Total expense ratio): An older name for a fund's total yearly running cost as a percentage. *Mechanics:* Largely replaced by the OCF in UK and European disclosure, though still used informally and for ETFs. *Why it matters:* You will see both terms; they are close but not identical.
- **Third-party commitment**: A client's promise to provide money to a private fund when the manager calls for it. *Mechanics:* GS reports commitments raised ($115bn in 2025) separately from AUS; some become fee-earning AUS only 'if and when the commitments are drawn and assets are invested'. *Why it matters:* Keeps a fundraising headline apart from funded, fee-paying assets.
- **Third-party distributed**: Client assets that come to GS funds through other firms, such as banks, wealth managers and platforms, and not from GS's own wealth clients. *Mechanics:* One of three GS client channels for long-term AUS. $714bn at 30 Sep 2026, up from $526bn a year earlier; net inflows of $97bn in the first nine months of 2026, which include acquired ETF assets. *Why it matters:* This is the disclosure line closest to what a third-party wealth team works on, at global level only.
- **TPE** (Third-party evaluator): An independent adviser that helps trustees choose and then monitor a fiduciary manager. *Mechanics:* Runs the tender, compares providers on a like-for-like basis and reports on ongoing performance. *Why it matters:* An influential gatekeeper in OCIO sales.
- **TPR** (The Pensions Regulator): The UK public body that protects workplace pensions. *Mechanics:* Regulates trustees and employers, sets codes of practice, authorises master trusts and oversees fiduciary management tendering duties. *Why it matters:* Its guidance shapes what trustees ask of managers and advisers.
- **TPW** (Third-party wealth): Selling an asset manager's funds to other firms' wealth businesses, which then use them for their own clients. *Mechanics:* A label used by some firms for wholesale distribution to private banks, wealth managers and advisers. The official name of any Goldman Sachs team should be checked against the teams chapter. *Why it matters:* One of the reported client-segment teams in the group this project studies.
- **Tracking error**: How differently a portfolio behaves from its benchmark. *Mechanics:* The annualised standard deviation of the difference in returns. Often capped in guidelines. *Why it matters:* Tells a client how much active risk they are paying for.
- **Transition management**: A specialist service that moves a portfolio from one manager or strategy to another at controlled cost and risk. *Mechanics:* Plans the trades, keeps market exposure during the move and reports the cost against a benchmark. *Why it matters:* Used when mandates are won, lost or restructured; the cost can decide whether a switch is worthwhile.
- **Trustee**: A person legally responsible for running a pension scheme or charity in the interests of its beneficiaries. *Mechanics:* Must take proper advice, follow the scheme's rules and act prudently. Many are lay people; professional trustees are increasingly common. *Why it matters:* The decision-maker in most UK pension relationships, with limited time and personal accountability.
- **UCITS** (Undertakings for Collective Investment in Transferable Securities): The standard regulated investment fund sold across Europe and the UK. *Mechanics:* A common rulebook on diversification, liquidity, custody and disclosure. Usually daily dealing. *Why it matters:* Most funds sold through wealth intermediaries are UCITS.
- **VNAV** (Variable net asset value): A money market fund whose share price moves with the market value of what it holds. *Mechanics:* No stable price promise; can be short-term or standard. *Why it matters:* An alternative where stable pricing is not required.
- **Volatility**: How much an investment's returns bounce around. *Mechanics:* Usually the annualised standard deviation of returns. Understates risk for assets that are valued infrequently. *Why it matters:* The most quoted risk number, and often not the one the client should care about most.
- **Wholesale distribution**: Selling funds to professional intermediaries instead of directly to institutions or the public. *Mechanics:* Covers private banks, wealth managers, advisers, platforms and funds of funds. *Why it matters:* The general industry term for the third-party wealth channel.
- **Yield**: The annual return an investment is expected to pay, as a percentage of its price. *Mechanics:* For bonds, yield moves opposite to price. *Why it matters:* Rates and yields drive pension funding, insurance demand and cash balances.

| W2-C01 | UK occupational pension scheme trustees must invest scheme assets in the best interests of members and beneficiaries, and in their sole interest where there is a potential conflict of interest. | Verified | W2-S01 |
| W2-C02 | Before investing, trustees must obtain and consider proper advice on whether the investment is satisfactory, and must decide how often to take such advice on investments they keep. | Verified | W2-S02 |
| W2-C03 | Trustees may delegate investment discretion to a fund manager and are not responsible for the manager's acts or defaults only if they have taken all reasonable steps to satisfy themselves about the manager's knowledge, experience and competence. | Verified | W2-S03 |
| W2-C04 | Trustees must run a qualifying competitive tender, with bids from at least three unconnected providers, when selecting fiduciary management providers for 20% or more of scheme assets; the duty has sat in pensions regulations overseen by The Pensions Regulator since 1 October 2022. | Verified | W2-S04 |
| W2-C05 | The CMA found in 2018 that firms providing both investment consultancy and fiduciary management had an incumbency advantage, that half of schemes bought fiduciary management from their existing consultant, and that some trustees showed low engagement in choosing and monitoring providers. | Verified | W2-S05 |
| W2-C06 | For investments, the FCA Consumer Duty applies to business with customers who are not professional clients, but it also reaches firms that determine or materially influence retail customer outcomes anywhere in the distribution chain, even without a direct relationship. | Verified | W2-S06 |
| W2-C07 | FCA rule COBS 2.1.1R requires a firm to act honestly, fairly and professionally in accordance with the best interests of its client. | Supported inference | W2-S07 |
| W2-C08 | LCP counted 367 UK pension buy-ins totalling £38.2bn in 2025, after £49.1bn in 2023 and £47.8bn in 2024. | Verified | W2-S08 |
